The actual truth of the allegations, official immunity, foreign-affairs defenses and the surviving torts' elements likewise are not necessary to this disposition.

**Precedent treatment:** Lewis v. Casey is extended from actual hindrance of current litigation to the necessary injury and remedial connection for an asserted lost past opportunity; Swierkiewicz retains ordinary notice pleading.

### The clarified theory also fails to identify distinct available relief

**Holding and operative rule:** Even treating counsel's intentional-infliction theory and lost emergency injunction as part of the pleaded case, this access count identifies no presently attainable remedy unavailable through the surviving tort action. That independently sufficient defect requires dismissal of the revived count.

**Authority:** The same eight Justices join this alternative holding; Thomas's judgment agreement supplies no additional rationale.

**Controlling explanation:** The requested emergency intervention could no longer save Harbury's husband when this suit was brought; that relief is unavailable under either theory. The identified intentional-infliction claim against the CIA defendants remains pleaded in counts 18 and 19. If it can be maintained, relief for its demonstrated consequences remains potentially available through that claim, and Harbury identifies no additional presently obtainable access remedy. This is an examination of the actual clarified cause and requested relief, not a presumption that all torts with similar labels compensate identical harm. If the predicate fails for a reason independent of the alleged deception, that failure cannot establish a viable underlying action for access purposes. Intentional deception can impede legal action, but it does not automatically create an additional damages channel for the same alleged consequences. Merely naming the same tort on amendment does not answer the independently adjudicated defect. Ordinary amendment rules remain applicable to any properly sought amendment on its actual allegations; the mandate orders none.

**Precedent treatment:** Lewis requires injury-fitted relief, not a duplicative access label. Malesko supplies no automatic new federal damages channel from an allegation of serious injury.

- Lewis v. Casey, June 24, 1996: its concrete-injury and remedial-fit principles extend to the asserted retrospective obstruction; the underlying cause and lost relief must be identified.
- Swierkiewicz v. Sorema N.A., February 26, 2002: ordinary notice pleading remains; substantive identification does not become a heightened evidentiary pleading rule.
- Correctional Services Corp. v. Malesko, November 27, 2001: its confined corporate-custody cause does not decide this different proposed damages channel; the latter is assumed, not recognized, here.

A backward-looking access theory cannot proceed without the identified arguable underlying action, causally lost opportunity and distinct available relief. The present complaint fails both identification and, even as clarified, the distinct-remedy requirement. Recognition of the assumed constitutional damages category remains unnecessary to the judgment.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#christopher-v-harbury-merits-2002-06-20).

### City of Columbus v. Ours Garage & Wrecker Service, Inc. — 2002-06-20

### A State may delegate its preserved motor-vehicle safety authority

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 14501(c)(2)(A) preserves a State's ability to authorize political subdivisions to exercise its safety regulatory authority with respect to motor vehicles. A local rule must actually fall within that delegated safety authority; an economic restriction does not qualify merely because the municipality calls it safety regulation.

**Authority:** Kennedy's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer, controls for seven Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** The general preemption clause expressly covers state and local laws relating to motor-carrier prices, routes or services. The safety exception preserves an identified state power. Mortier explains that a State ordinarily exercises retained regulatory power through authorized subdivisions; naming the State does not alone forbid that internal allocation. The contrary inference from Congress's separate references to subdivisions elsewhere is substantial but not conclusive: the exceptions preserve different subjects, and a preserved state authority is not naturally restricted to personal exercise by state officials. The statute's separate protection for vehicle size or weight and hazardous-cargo route controls, and for minimum financial responsibility or insurance, retains its distinct scope. These provisions do not transform all towing regulation into safety regulation. Economic deregulation remains effective because the court must examine what each ordinance actually regulates; Section 31141's federal supervisory process likewise remains operative. The Sixth Circuit stopped at categorical institutional exclusion and did not decide the necessary ordinance-specific questions. It must examine Ohio's authorization and the actual safety character of each challenged requirement, together with any independent federal conflict.

**Precedent treatment:** Wisconsin Public Intervenor v. Mortier, June 21, 1991, supplies the ordinary state-delegation principle; the federal statute still controls the scope of saved regulation and actual preemption.

- Wisconsin Public Intervenor v. Mortier, 501 U.S. 597 (1991): applied to the State's ordinary use of authorized subdivisions when Congress preserves a state regulatory power.
- Medtronic, Inc. v. Lohr, June 26, 1996: clause-specific analysis remains; saving a regulatory subject does not validate every state or local requirement.
- Geier v. American Honda Motor Co., May 22, 2000: independently established actual federal conflict remains a separate question; the delegation holding removes no such ground.

Authorized municipalities may exercise the State's saved motor-vehicle safety authority. Neither independent municipal sovereignty nor a general local economic-regulation exception follows. Each challenged provision requires its own authorization, safety and remaining-preemption analysis.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#city-of-columbus-v-ours-garage-wrecker-service-inc-merits-2002-06-20).

### Dadian v. Village of Wilmette — 2002-06-20

### Necessary reasonable access includes a supported exception to a driveway rule

**Holding and operative rule:** Under the FHAA and ADA Title II, a municipality must make a reasonable modification of its driveway restriction when the plaintiffs establish that it is necessary to afford a qualified resident equal housing access, subject to an established applicable defense. Actual usability for the particular resident governs necessity; a preferred or more valuable design alone is insufficient, and a genuinely equally effective alternative can defeat the asserted need.

**Authority:** Scalia's opinion is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer; all nine adopt the complete accommodation rule and application.

**Controlling explanation:** The statutes require an individual examination of the obstacle caused by disability. Mrs. Dadian's longstanding walking limitations and the evidence comparing the longer rear route with shorter front access permitted a rational jury to find disability, necessity and reasonableness. The existence of some driveway did not establish equal usable access, and the rear design was not conclusively equivalent as a matter of law. Martin confirms that uniform rules and institutional preference do not replace the statutory individual inquiry. Toyota's attention to substantial limitation and daily activities does not turn this tried walking evidence into a pleading defect; Barnett's employment seniority conflict is absent. The Village's appraisal and other evidentiary objections do not justify a new trial: the challenged material bore on the comparison or impeachment, and any error was harmless against the principal medical, personal and meeting evidence. Because accommodation independently supports the same relief, the Court need not decide whether intentional discrimination was independently proved. The property-specific injunction follows from this record rather than a general entitlement to front driveways.

**Precedent treatment:** PGA TOUR, Inc. v. Martin supplies individual statutory assessment, while its Title III fundamental-alteration defense remains distinct. Toyota and Barnett retain their separate definitions and employment context.

### The Village bears its objective safety defense; this instructional error was harmless

**Holding and operative rule:** A municipality asserting that the accommodation presents a direct threat must prove, through objective individualized evidence, a significant risk that reasonable accommodation cannot eliminate. Safety applies to accommodation as well as intentional discrimination; confining the instruction to the latter was error, but this jury's actual rejection of the same asserted threat makes that placement error harmless.

**Authority:** All nine join the same Court opinion on defense allocation, the error and its record-specific harmlessness.

**Controlling explanation:** Bragdon requires an objective assessment rather than stereotypes or unsupported fear. The Village may protect children and pedestrians, but must connect its asserted turning and traffic danger to the actual driveway, driver and possible mitigation. Impaired walking does not itself prove unsafe driving. Assigning the affirmative safety defense to the Village leaves the Dadians responsible for disability, necessity and reasonable accommodation. The instruction should have applied to accommodation, yet the jury necessarily rejected the asserted danger under the theory to which it was given. Repeating that same determination under the accommodation label would not correct a consequential error here. This is not a rule that every favorable verdict cures an omitted instruction: harmlessness depends on this verdict's resolution of this defense.

**Precedent treatment:** Bragdon v. Abbott supplies objective individual assessment and the defense's evidentiary basis; Echazabal's workplace self-threat rule is not imported into municipal housing law.

- Bragdon v. Abbott, June 25, 1998: objective risk assessment is applied to the asserted municipal direct-threat defense; unsupported assumptions do not suffice.
- PGA TOUR, Inc. v. Martin, May 29, 2001: individual assessment under the applicable accommodation provision is applied; its distinct Title III fundamental-alteration defense remains separate.
- Toyota Motor Manufacturing, Kentucky, Inc. v. Williams, January 8, 2002: substantial limitation remains an individual inquiry; this record supplies evidence of longstanding walking limitations.
- US Airways, Inc. v. Barnett, April 29, 2002: necessity and reasonableness remain distinct; the employment seniority conflict does not govern this physical-access exception.
- Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Echazabal, June 10, 2002: the objective method does not convert its workplace self-threat regulation into the governing municipal defense.
- Barnes v. Gorman, June 17, 2002: its remedial holding does not alter the property-specific injunction or create a new damages question here.

Necessary reasonable driveway modifications remain enforceable under the FHAA and ADA Title II on individual proof. The Village must substantiate an asserted direct threat; objective safety and effective alternatives remain relevant. The existing front-driveway permit relief stands without a general land-use exemption.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#dadian-v-village-of-wilmette-merits-2002-06-20).

### Gonzaga University v. Doe — 2002-06-20

### These FERPA nondisclosure provisions do not support the asserted Section 1983 claim

**Holding and operative rule:** FERPA's education-record nondisclosure provisions do not establish the individual judicially enforceable entitlement asserted in this Section 1983 action. The FERPA-based federal judgment must therefore be reversed; the state-action determination, excluded from review, and independent state-law judgments are unaffected.

**Authority:** Six Justices agree on this statute-specific conclusion. Thomas's lead opinion is joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy; Breyer, joined by Souter, concurs in the judgment on the separate FERPA-specific explanation below. No majority adopts a new general formula equating a statutory right with intent to create a private remedy.

**Controlling explanation:** Section 1983 requires an individual federal right, not merely an asserted statutory violation. Six Justices conclude that these FERPA provisions do not establish the individual judicially enforceable entitlement asserted here, so this federal component cannot stand. Their agreement does not extend to one general formula for finding statutory rights or to a single full explanation of this statute. The four-Justice lead and the two-Justice concurrence supply their own reasons, stated separately. The holding concerns these nondisclosure provisions; it neither categorically excludes funding legislation nor makes the absence of an implied statutory action dispositive. Because this threshold fails, a separate damages ruling is unnecessary.

**Precedent treatment:** Blessing and Bruneau remain distinct provision-specific right and enforcement inquiries; Owasso's reservation of enforcement is resolved for these nondisclosure provisions. No general rule supported only by the four-Justice lead is elevated into controlling law.

- Blessing v. Freestone, April 21, 1997: applied through the particular asserted statutory entitlement, not a generalized interest in sound administration.
- Suter v. Artist M., March 25, 1992: its provision-specific distinction between enforceable entitlement and institutional obligation remains effective.
- Bruneau v. South Kortright Central School District, June 3, 1999: an established individual Title IX right remains enforceable through Section 1983 absent incompatible displacement; that established right does not create a missing FERPA right.
- Owasso Independent School District No. I-011 v. Falvo, February 19, 2002: its definition of education records remains; it did not resolve private enforcement.
- Powell v. Ridge, April 3, 2000, and Alexander v. Sandoval, April 24, 2001: their distinct regulation and statutory-channel holdings are not substituted for the right-creation inquiry here.
- Barnes v. Gorman, June 17, 2002: its incorporated remedial channels do not supply a Section 1983 damages ruling in this threshold disposition.

The asserted FERPA nondisclosure claim cannot proceed through Section 1983 because these provisions do not establish the required judicially enforceable entitlement. Existing law continues to distinguish right creation, the enacted Section 1983 channel, implied statutory actions and incompatible remedial displacement. No broader rights formula commands five votes in this decision.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#gonzaga-university-v-doe-merits-2002-06-20).

### Page v. Bartels — 2002-06-20

### The constitutional inquiry concerns a protected interest and the operation impairing it

**Holding and operative rule:** Racial consideration or predominance in a common territorial electorate does not itself establish a constitutional deprivation. The constitutional claims presented here must identify the governmental operation impairing a protected political interest and satisfy that claim's own elements; voters of every race receive the same protection, and Section 2 claims proceed independently on their statutory elements.

**Authority:** Part I of the signed opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five direct votes. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas do not join this part.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court reaffirms the boundary drawn by Shaw, Miller and Easley. A common territorial electorate places residents in the same geographical constituency without different legal eligibility because of individual race. Racial motivation explains why boundaries were chosen; the relevant operation identifies what those boundaries did to the protected interest. Composition, shape and predominance may supply evidence, but cannot replace proof of an actual exclusion, intentional dilution, materially unequal participation or other independently recognized wrong. Conversely, a shared electorate does not excuse a proved denial of equal access. Intentional dilution requires purpose, material impairment of protected electoral opportunity and causation. A participation claim requires an identified protected activity and concrete material impairment attributable to the challenged racial treatment or prohibited purpose; it does not require an already lost election. A material impairment is a practical restriction on the protected ability to participate or elect; total exclusion is unnecessary, while a technical change or disappointment about political leverage alone is insufficient. Those distinctions require separate attention to the actual dilution and chilling claims rather than allowing the lower court's nonpredominance reasoning to answer them. This case applies the existing boundary to those claims; it adopts no comprehensive new test for unpresented partisan or regional-separation theories.

**Precedent treatment:** Shaw v. Reno, Johnson v. Miller and Easley v. Cromartie: reaffirmed at their established territorial-design boundary and protection of independent injuries. The actual statutory inquiry remains governed by Section 2 and its own precedents.

### The statutory dilution claim fails on the relevant opposing-bloc evidence

**Holding and operative rule:** Section 2 does not require retention of a numerical-majority district where, on supported findings concerning the affected electorate and relevant nomination and election stages, challengers fail to establish opposing-bloc voting ordinarily sufficient to defeat the protected group's preferred candidates after considering relevant crossover support and special circumstances. Failure of that necessary condition disposes of this claim without deciding the first capacity condition or the remaining totality inquiry.

**Authority:** Part II of the signed opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven direct votes. Scalia and Thomas agree only in the statutory judgment on their noncoverage ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that actual electoral opportunity, rather than a fixed racial percentage, governs the asserted loss. Gingles supplies the necessary voting conditions, and Voinovich permits disposition when the relevant opposing-bloc showing fails. Valdespino preserves both the numerical-majority route and rigorous proof of dependable crossover capacity, but neither route eliminates this separate requirement. Plaintiffs retain the ordinary preponderance burden: the required proposition must be more likely true than not. Valdespino's rigorous-support requirement concerns reliability, not a heightened proof standard or mandatory research protocol. The trial court credited an analysis of more than 150 elections, distinguished relevant contests and candidate preferences, and examined projected performance in each affected district. It also considered the Democratic nomination stage and evidence of African-American primary participation. Those findings support opportunity in both Districts 27 and 34 even without Hispanic support. The contrary expert evidence was received and rejected for identified methodological and contextual reasons; clear-error review does not authorize a new appellate credibility trial. The reduction from 52.8% to 27.5% Black voting-age population in District 27 therefore does not itself prove loss of opportunity. Possible gains elsewhere or the race of successful candidates would not answer that question, but the judgment rests on local preference, crossover and nomination evidence. De Grandy rejects both maximization and conclusive proportionality; it supplies no statewide offset for a local deprivation. No error in the independently sufficient findings requires remand. Distinct-group aggregation and influence-only entitlement remain undecided because neither is necessary after this condition fails.

**Precedent treatment:** Gingles and Voinovich: applied to the independently necessary opposing-bloc condition. Valdespino: both capacity routes remain intact without a new capacity determination. De Grandy: preserved against maximization, numerical shortcuts and statewide-offset reasoning. Balderas: its separate record and fractured application supply no substitute for these identified findings.

### The intentional statutory claim also fails on this record

**Holding and operative rule:** The separately pleaded intentional Section 2 claim fails because supported findings establish neither purposeful diminution of minority voting rights nor the actual impairment asserted. This ground is separate from the results claim and does not make intent necessary for a Section 2 results violation.

**Authority:** Part II of the signed opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven direct votes. Scalia and Thomas agree only in the statutory judgment on their noncoverage ground.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court addresses Count II on its own terms. The trial court rejected the asserted purpose to diminish minority electoral opportunity and separately rejected the alleged loss and participation impairment. Those findings remain supported under clear-error review. Racial awareness and changing population shares do not themselves establish the pleaded purposeful diminution. The Court therefore affirms this statutory count without treating the results count as an intent claim or using lack of racial predominance as a required element.

**Precedent treatment:** The existing Section 2 results framework remains independent. Voinovich preserves claim-specific proof and does not make all statutory protection depend on purpose.

### The actual dilution and participation theories fail on supported findings

**Holding and operative rule:** Intentional dilution and materially unequal political participation require proof of the unlawful purpose or treatment, material impairment and causal connection belonging to the actual claim. The supported findings rejecting both loss of electoral opportunity and the separately alleged chilling of participation defeat these claims; the absence of established discriminatory purpose provides an additional ground.

**Authority:** The constitutional application is joined by all nine Justices: Part III addresses the Fourteenth Amendment and Part IV the Fifteenth Amendment. This complete agreement on the confined applications does not enlarge the five-Justice coalition for Part I.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that neither demographic redistribution nor racial attention alone establishes the constitutional wrong alleged here. Easley preserves independently asserted intentional dilution and unequal-participation claims under their own elements. The trial addressed these independent theories. Supported local electoral findings defeat the dilution allegation, and the court separately rejected the predicted chilling of African-American and Hispanic participation. That second finding matters: a preferred candidate's ability to win does not universally answer an obstruction of political access. The court also found no purpose to diminish minority voting rights. Plaintiffs bear the ordinary preponderance burden on their elements. A prohibited purpose need not be the sole or predominant motive; action partly because it would impair equal electoral opportunity or participation is distinct from mere awareness of effects. Arlington Heights and Feeney distinguish awareness of racial consequences from a prohibited purpose; Brooks requires the government, once prohibited purpose is established as a substantial or motivating factor, to prove by a preponderance of the evidence that materially the same decision would have occurred without that purpose. The threshold predicate is not established here. The lower court's additional reliance on lack of racial predominance does not supply the governing test, but its independent findings sustain this judgment. No developed separate due-process theory is presented for decision.

**Precedent treatment:** Easley: its protection of independently asserted dilution and participation claims is applied; no reconsideration of the distinct territorial-design rule is necessary. Arlington Heights, Feeney and Brooks: their purpose and burden rules remain intact; no established prohibited-purpose predicate triggers the latter burden here.

### The Fifteenth Amendment claim fails without deciding dilution coverage

**Holding and operative rule:** Assuming the Fifteenth Amendment reaches the asserted dilution theory, the supported absence of purposeful racial abridgment defeats this claim. The Court leaves the broader coverage question open and affirms the denial of relief on this component.

**Authority:** The constitutional application is joined by all nine Justices: Part III addresses the Fourteenth Amendment and Part IV the Fifteenth Amendment. This complete agreement on the confined applications does not enlarge the five-Justice coalition for Part I.

**Controlling explanation:** Voinovich permits rejection of an assumed dilution claim for failed proof of prohibited purpose while reserving the Amendment's coverage. The completed trial supplies that independent ground here. Awareness of racial composition and a change in numerical concentration do not displace the supported finding that the plan was not designed to diminish minority voting rights. The Court therefore does not adopt the lower court's categorical suggestion that legislative dilution falls outside the Fifteenth Amendment. This constitutional purpose inquiry does not convert Section 2's separate enacted results standard into an intent requirement.

**Precedent treatment:** Voinovich: applied for the assumed-coverage, failed-purpose route; the Fifteenth Amendment coverage reservation remains effective.

- Thornburg v. Gingles: applied to the distinct voting conditions, subject to Valdespino's change to numerical exclusivity.
- Valdespino v. Alamo Heights Independent School District (June 17, 1999): both the numerical-majority and proved dependable-crossover capacity routes remain available; the separate opposing-bloc condition resolves this results claim.
- Growe v. Emison and Voinovich v. Quilter: the causal voting requirements, claimant's burden and distinction between state choice and a court-compelled remedy remain intact. Voinovich's Fifteenth Amendment coverage reservation is preserved.
- Johnson v. De Grandy: rejection of maximization and conclusive ratios remains effective; local deprivation cannot be offset by gains elsewhere, and totality remains independent when reached.
- Balderas v. Texas (June 17, 2002): its fractured summary affirmance does not create a finding-sufficiency shortcut or decide this different record.
- Shaw v. Reno, Johnson v. Miller and Easley v. Cromartie: Part I reaffirms the territorial-design boundary and protection for independently established wrongs. The confined constitutional applications receive the separate joins stated above.
- Arlington Heights, Feeney and Brooks v. Barnes: prohibited purpose remains distinct from racial awareness, and the government's applicable same-decision burden follows an established substantial or motivating prohibited purpose; no such predicate is established here.

The governing statutory framework permits changes to racial population shares when the actual results claim fails a necessary electoral condition; it creates neither a numerical safe harbor nor a right to maximize seats. The separately pleaded intentional statutory theory and the constitutional claims remain distinct, each resolved on its own supported failure of proof. Constitutional protection extends equally to every race and preserves material participation injury independently of election success. The existing territorial-design boundary remains unchanged.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#page-v-bartels-summary-2002-06-20).

### Robertson v. Bartels — 2002-06-20

### Political alignment and common requested relief do not bind these nonparties

**Holding and operative rule:** A prior judgment cannot bind different plaintiffs merely because they share political interests, request the same relief, know of the earlier case or could have sought intervention. The stated virtual-representation ground fails without constitutionally sufficient consent, control, genuine privity, authorized representation or another valid binding arrangement; agreement to admit testimony supplies none of those relationships by itself.

**Authority:** Part I of the signed opinion of the Court is joined by all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Page judgment does not bind these plaintiffs on the reasons the district court gave. Richards requires lawful authority to represent an absent person, and Hansberry preserves the due-process prerequisite to genuine representative adjudication. Shared political association, vigorous advocacy, adequate incentives and the public importance of a controversy do not establish that authority. Nor does describing the second suit as tactical create a general duty to intervene. The parties agreed to the use of Page testimony as evidence; they did not thereby agree to its inferences, authorize representation or consent to binding adjudication. The public character of map relief does not, without an actual authorized arrangement, turn ordinary litigation into a proceeding representing all interested voters. Devlin concerns persons already bound as members of a mandatory class and does not make these strangers members of one. Valid representative and special remedial arrangements remain possible, but none is supplied by the stated rationale. The independent merits ground permits disposition without an exploratory remand to construct a new preclusion relationship.

**Precedent treatment:** Richards and Hansberry: applied to the necessary authority for binding absent claimants. Devlin: distinguished because its appellants were already bound mandatory-class members, not political allies outside the litigation.

### The territorial-design count identifies no independent constitutional deprivation

**Holding and operative rule:** Predominantly racial design of a common territorial electorate does not alone establish a Fourteenth Amendment deprivation. Even giving these plaintiffs the favorable reasonable inferences required on summary judgment, Count I identifies no independently cognizable denial or abridgment; the judgment denying relief on that count therefore stands.

**Authority:** Parts II and III of the signed opinion of the Court are joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five direct votes. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas dissent from the merits disposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the disputed weight of racial considerations is not material to the design-only claim under governing law. Shaw, Miller and Easley distinguish the motive for common district boundaries from an actual exclusion, dilution, unequal participation or other prohibited treatment. The Court assumes favorable reasonable inferences from Bartels's minority-incumbent protection commitment and does not decide that race could not have predominated merely because other criteria were also considered or the map had ordinary shape. Anderson v. Liberty Lobby requires those favorable inferences, but an inference about motive cannot supply a missing legal deprivation. The dissent would reconsider the governing boundary and treat predominant racial assignment itself as unequal treatment. The Court retains the distinction: membership in the resulting common electorate imposes no racial qualification or legally unequal civic status by itself. That conclusion requires neither a second governmental act nor electoral defeat or financial loss. The actual territorial theory also supplies no independent denial or abridgment for the pleaded Fifteenth Amendment theory; its broader dilution coverage need not be decided.

**Precedent treatment:** Shaw, Miller and Easley: applied without changing the territorial-design boundary or protections for actual independent wrongs. Anderson v. Liberty Lobby: applied to favorable reasonable inferences, with materiality determined by the governing substantive law. Voinovich: its broader Fifteenth Amendment coverage reservation remains intact.

### The actual incumbent-prospect claim concerns the same territorial operation

**Holding and operative rule:** A claim that district boundaries favor minority incumbents' reelection prospects does not become a separate racial allocation of candidacy or civic rights merely by describing those prospects as an incumbent benefit. Count II identifies the composition of the common electorate and its expected political support, without an independent eligibility restriction or participation impairment, and fails under the same constitutional boundary.

**Authority:** Parts II and III of the signed opinion of the Court are joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: five direct votes. O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas dissent from the merits disposition.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the actual governmental operation, not the beneficiary label, determines this claim. The evidence of a commitment to protect minority incumbents receives its favorable inference; the Court neither approves every such preference nor excuses established racial discrimination because no incumbent owns a future term. Here the challenged benefit consists of the reelection prospects resulting from district lines. The record supplies no separate racial ballot qualification, restricted candidacy, office entitlement, official racial ballot label or independent participation barrier in Counts I or II. Anderson v. Martin concerns official racial electoral labeling and therefore addresses a different operation. Adarand supplies justification requirements for an actual covered racial allocation; it does not itself supply the missing deprivation here. Easley preserves review of unequal eligibility and materially unequal participation without demanding a changed winner, but those are not the actual basis of this count. The separate residency controversy in Count III cannot be borrowed to enlarge this appeal. Because the asserted benefit remains the same territorial composition, the disputed priority of motives does not require a trial under the governing rule.

**Precedent treatment:** Easley: applied to the actual operation and preserved independent injuries. Anderson v. Martin: distinguished from common geographical composition. Adarand: preserved for an actual covered racial allocation, without using its justification rule to create one.

- Richards v. Jefferson County: applied to lawful authority to bind absent claimants; political affinity, common objectives and failure to intervene supply no substitute. Its allowance for actual authorized representative and special remedial arrangements remains intact.
- Hansberry v. Lee: genuine representation must meet due process.
- Devlin v. Scardelletti (June 10, 2002): distinguished because its mandatory-class members were actually bound; it creates no representative relationship among these political allies.
- Shaw v. Reno, Johnson v. Miller and Easley v. Cromartie: the territorial-design boundary is reaffirmed for both the direct design claim and the actual electorate-based incumbent-prospect theory. Their protection of independently cognizable wrongs remains intact.
- Anderson v. Liberty Lobby: favorable reasonable inferences are preserved; materiality depends on the substantive cause.
- Anderson v. Martin: official racial electoral labeling differs from the common geographical electorate challenged here.
- Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña: justification rules for an actual covered racial allocation remain effective and do not supply a missing deprivation.
- Voinovich v. Quilter: the broader Fifteenth Amendment dilution-coverage question remains reserved.

The nonparty rule remains grounded in actual authority to bind a person's rights. The constitutional territorial-design rule covers the actual incumbent-prospect theory when the claimed benefit consists of the same district composition; it does not immunize a different racial allocation of eligibility, civic status or participation. Both claims can be rejected while assuming favorable racial-motive inferences, without resolving the disputed relative weight of motives or endorsing every rationale below.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#robertson-v-bartels-summary-2002-06-20).

### Rush Prudential HMO, Inc. v. Moran — 2002-06-20

### Independent medical review regulates this insured coverage

**Holding and operative rule:** Illinois HMO Act §4-10 is saved from ERISA preemption as applied to this risk-bearing HMO: when the primary care physician and HMO disagree about the necessity of a covered service the physician proposes, it requires timely independent physician review and provision of the service if found medically necessary. The rule supplies an enforceable insured-coverage condition; the three insurance guideposts are not indispensable conjunctive requirements.

**Authority:** The signed opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls with six votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that furnishing medical care does not erase the insurance function of an HMO that assumes its members' medical-cost risk. Metropolitan Life permits States to regulate insurance-contract terms, and UNUM v. Ward applies that principle to a mandatory condition governing payment. Section 4-10 directs its command to insured HMO coverage and makes independent medical assessment part of the policy relationship. Those industry and policy-relationship features confirm the common-sense classification as insurance regulation; the Court need not decide whether independent review also independently transfers risk. The law therefore cannot be dismissed as ordinary regulation of health care merely because the insurer arranges treatment as well as paying for it. This holding concerns the insured arrangement before the Court and leaves the deemer clause's protection of self-funded plans intact.

**Precedent treatment:** Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Massachusetts: applied to substantive insurance-contract regulation. UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward: extended from notice-prejudice to this limited medical-necessity term, preserving its nonconjunctive guideposts and separate conflict inquiry.

### The saved medical determination remains enforceable through ERISA

**Holding and operative rule:** The binding physician determination under §4-10 is compatible with ERISA because it supplies the medical-necessity predicate for existing covered benefits while judicial enforcement remains under §502(a)(1)(B). It neither creates an independent state cause of action nor authorizes consequential or punitive damages, and ERISA does not guarantee that an insurer may preserve contractual medical discretion against an otherwise saved insurance condition.

**Authority:** The signed opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, controls with six votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that a mandatory medical assessment can define what the insurer owes without creating a competing remedy. Pilot Life bars an alternative state enforcement scheme; Moran instead seeks the benefits promised by her insured coverage through ERISA's own action. The unaffiliated reviewer holds the same class of professional license as the primary care physician and resolves the limited medical-necessity question, rather than awarding additional relief or exercising general arbitral jurisdiction. Firestone governs review of discretion a plan actually possesses; it does not entitle an insurer to retain discretion that valid insurance regulation removes. UNUM likewise allows a saved policy condition to inform a federal benefits action while separately rejecting an administrative reassignment rule on its own ground. Rush's strongest objection—that binding review necessarily substitutes an outside adjudication for the federal scheme—thus overlooks the limited determination and unchanged remedy here. Great-West construes the distinct §502(a)(3) equitable channel and does not restrict the benefits action Moran invokes. The reviewer's favorable medical-necessity decision supports the existing benefits judgment. The Court does not decide an unraised proper-defendant objection, the proper initial vehicle for compelling review, or systems resolving different coverage questions. The authority of this reviewer does not settle a separate experimental-treatment exclusion or a challenge to biased or inaccurate review, which are outside the presented dispute.

**Precedent treatment:** Pilot Life Insurance Co. v. Dedeaux: distinguished because no competing state remedy is supplied. Firestone Tire & Rubber Co. v. Bruch: applied to actual contractual discretion, without converting it into immunity from insurance regulation. UNUM: applied to compatible federal enforcement of saved coverage terms. Great-West Life & Annuity Insurance Co. v. Knudson: distinguished as addressing §502(a)(3), not §502(a)(1)(B).

- Metropolitan Life Insurance Co. v. Massachusetts: substantive insurance-contract regulation remains protected by the saving clause.
- UNUM Life Insurance Co. of America v. Ward, No. 97-1868 (Apr. 20, 1999): its saved-condition rule extends to this medical-necessity term; its separate administrative-connection holding and actual-conflict inquiry remain intact.
- Pilot Life Insurance Co. v. Dedeaux: its bar on competing state enforcement remains effective; the limited medical review does not supply such a remedy.
- Firestone Tire & Rubber Co. v. Bruch: its review rule concerns discretion lawfully retained by the plan and does not guarantee discretion against saved policy regulation.
- Great-West Life & Annuity Insurance Co. v. Knudson, No. 99-1786 (Jan. 8, 2002): its §502(a)(3) limits remain unchanged and do not alter this benefits action.

The saved-insurance-condition rule now includes this mandatory independent determination of medical necessity within insured coverage. The resulting benefit is enforceable under §502(a)(1)(B), with the federal remedial limits unchanged. Neither HMO status nor the label independent review creates a general exemption from ERISA; the operation of another system and any actual federal conflict require their own analysis.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#rush-prudential-hmo-inc-v-moran-merits-2002-06-20).

### Utah v. Evans — 2002-06-20

### Completed transmission does not defeat redress of the representation injury

**Holding and operative rule:** A State has standing to contest a census method that causes an identified loss of a House seat when authoritative relief concerning the responsible census determination would likely correct the allocation through the statutory sequence. Completed transmission and certification do not categorically bar correction of court-determined legal error in the count.

**Authority:** Part I of the opinion of the Court is joined by all nine Justices.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Utah's identified marginal-seat loss is a concrete injury caused by the challenged method. Franklin recognizes likely redress through correction of the responsible census determination; Wisconsin applies that principle to representational injury, and Department of Commerce requires concrete injury for the particular plaintiff. Utah meets those requirements. North Carolina's certificate objection would make a legally erroneous census input irreversible, but the reporting and allocation statutes contain no such categorical command. The Court reads their sequence to permit correction of the underlying determination and the resulting certificate for legal error. Likely redress follows from that official sequence, without a coercive presidential injunction or a general assumption that absent persons obey judicial advice. Whether Utah proves illegality is a merits question and does not erase its standing. The President's APA status and susceptibility to coercive relief are unnecessary to this holding and remain reserved.

**Precedent treatment:** Franklin v. Massachusetts: applied to likely redress through the allocation sequence. Wisconsin v. City of New York: applied to continuing representational injury. Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives: applied to plaintiff-specific injury rather than statutory authorization alone.

### The Census Act permits this residual completion method

**Holding and operative rule:** Sections 141 and 195 permit hot-deck imputation that completes particular unresolved units with nearby comparable information after genuine mail and field enumeration. This residual method does not select a representative sample to substitute for enumeration of a larger unenumerated population, and §195 does not prohibit it; its small percentage alone supplies no permission.

**Authority:** Part II of the opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: seven direct votes.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the method's actual operation fits the enacted enumeration authority. The Bureau attempts to identify and count units individually, then fills remaining address, occupancy and household-size gaps from nearby units of the same type. Treating unresolved entries as zero would itself infer an answer rather than count established persons more literally. Section 141 permits this supported completion of the count, and §195 does not withdraw that authority for this method. The contrary argument equates every inference from observed information with a representative sample. But this process completes identified residual units after attempted enumeration; it does not select a fraction of the population to stand for a larger unenumerated whole. The distinction rests on function, not merely timing, randomness or scale. Department of Commerce permits two particular sampling designs; its general permission-versus-duty construction of §195 did not command a majority. The Court therefore neither elevates that construction into law nor decides materially different sampling designs. The statutory claim fails on the described method itself.

**Precedent treatment:** Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives: its permission for two identified designs remains intact; the four-Justice general construction does not control this independent method-specific analysis.

### The explained method reasonably serves enumeration and geographic representation

**Holding and operative rule:** The Enumeration Clause permits this residual imputation because it supplements a genuine population-counting effort and bears an explained reasonable relationship to enumeration and population-based representation. Locality and comparable housing type support the inference on this record without proof of quantified geographic superiority; a national-total improvement alone, a fixed percentage or unsupported geographic assumptions would not supply that relationship.

**Authority:** Part III of the opinion of the Court is joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer: six direct votes. Thomas opposes; O'Connor and Scalia do not reach this component.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the Constitution does not require every counted fact to be personally observed. Department of Commerce rejects an exclusively physical-headcount rule, while Wisconsin requires a genuine enumeration and an explained relationship to population-based representation. The Bureau first attempted direct enumeration. For remaining gaps, it chose a geographically nearby donor of the same housing type and kept the unresolved unit at its own location. Those features connect the inference to geographic distribution rather than merely a larger national total. The Hogan declaration's numerical-accuracy priority and the absence of a study establishing improved distributive accuracy preclude a finding that this design was proved geographically superior. They do not establish that geography was ignored. The actual choice is between a supported local inference and treating unresolved entries as zero, which also carries geographically uneven error. Neither alternative guarantees perfection; the explained design satisfies reasonable-relationship review. The final-seat consequence does not change that standard. The small residual use is evidence of fit, not a constitutional safe harbor. Thomas's proposed count-based limit cannot supply the governing rule after Department of Commerce, though his disagreement remains stated separately. Discriminatory manipulation, unsupported geographic proxies and absence of a genuine enumeration effort remain outside this permission. No freestanding APA merits claim is before the Court.

**Precedent treatment:** Wisconsin v. City of New York: applied without changing its reasonable-relationship standard or requiring empirical certainty. Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives: applied for constitutional permission to supplement a genuine enumeration with supported inference. Franklin v. Massachusetts: its supported counting choices remain consistent with this application; its separate administrative-review rule is unchanged.

- Franklin v. Massachusetts, No. 91-1502 (June 26, 1992): applied to redress through correction of the responsible determination and allocation sequence; its independent finality and administrative-review rules remain unchanged.
- Wisconsin v. City of New York, Nos. 94-1614, 94-1631 and 94-1985 (Mar. 20, 1996): applied to continuing representational injury and explained methodological choice reasonably related to enumeration and geographic representation.
- Department of Commerce v. United States House of Representatives / Clinton v. Glavin, Nos. 98-404 and 98-564 (Jan. 25, 1999): applied to plaintiff-specific standing and constitutional permission for supported supplementation; its two-design statutory permission remains binding, while its general §195 construction remains a four-Justice position.

Completed apportionment certificates do not make the responsible census determination immune from correction of legal error. The Census Act and Enumeration Clause permit the described residual, geographically grounded completion of a genuine enumeration. The constitutional reasonable-relationship standard is applied without requiring proof of perfect or guaranteed improvement. The decision adds this method-specific statutory permission without settling the general construction of §195 or authorizing every inference method.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#utah-v-evans-merits-2002-06-20).

## 4. Material Published Noncontrolling Positions

### TRW Inc. v. Andrews — 2001-11-13

**Stone-Zsela, concurring.** Stone-Zsela joins the Court's statutory ruling and judgment. He adds that liability arises when the elements necessary for the particular statutory claim exist: rejection of general discovery does not erase the need for an element-specific inquiry or an independently applicable preserved exception. Rotella rejects substitution of a later discovery event for the governing accrual inquiry, but does not make disclosure, discovery and actionable injury interchangeable. Properly preserved questions remain subject to ordinary adjudication. The newly presented theory receives no affirmative permission to reopen merely because the Court leaves it undecided.

**Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurring in the judgment.** They begin with the traditional rule that a limitations period ordinarily runs when the cause of action is complete and enforceable, rather than when the claimant learns of it. A general discovery presumption does not follow from Congress's failure expressly to reject discovery. Congress can prescribe a different start and did enact a confined exception here. Traditional fraud/concealment protection and the latent-injury setting remain separate rules. They object that leaving the general background uncertain invites uncertainty under other statutes. Their agreement with reversal and remand does not establish that every disclosure completes every FCRA claim or decide the newly raised actual-loss theory.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#trw-inc-v-andrews-merits-2001-11-13).

### United States Postal Service v. Gregory — 2001-11-13

**Thomas, concurring.** Thomas joins the limited statutory opinion at the scope shown in the table, but would also decide statutory sufficiency because the Postal Service's reliance on Bolling makes that ground part of the presented dispute. Under Bolling, full fresh review applies if written notice, a recorded action, or an opportunity to challenge above the original imposing authority was absent. If all three exist, the employee may show clear error on the earlier record. Thomas considers that framework consistent with §7503's protections for minor discipline and its absence of a direct Board appeal. The statute does not require retrying the facts of every earlier action merely because it bears on a later penalty. The agency must establish the earlier action, while the appealed charge and penalty retain their own proof requirements. The contractual and Board routes protect different rights; one route's conclusion is not automatically a prerequisite for the other. He accepts remand concerning the overturned warning without commanding reopening. His affirmative statutory view neither becomes the Court's holding nor resolves every constitutional application.

**Ginsburg, concurring in the judgment.** Ginsburg questions whether restricted clear-error review can satisfy the statutory preponderance requirement when the earlier merits remain unadjudicated. The opportunity to contest a recorded action does not itself prove adequate review. She therefore withholds assent to the Court's independent-review rationale, without holding Bolling invalid or requiring fresh review of every final disciplinary action. Her remand rests on the Board's actual reopening power, Gregory's election of Board review of the removal, the reservation of Bolling's adequacy, and rejection of an absolute prohibition on agency consideration of pending discipline. Reopening provides a route to request consideration of the changed disciplinary record, not guaranteed relief. A refusal to address a material invalidated predicate could present an arbitrary-and-capricious question. The removal election does not abandon earlier grievances, establish personal control over union arbitration, or substitute for adequate Board process. Her distinction between final discipline and an action still being challenged remains a noncontrolling qualification.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#united-states-postal-service-v-gregory-merits-2001-11-13).

### Chickasaw Nation v. United States — 2001-11-27

**Souter, joined by Stone-Zsela and O'Connor, dissenting.** The dissent would give the expressly named chapter operative effect. The reporting-and-withholding description and the substantive chapter reference pull in different directions; calling the reference an accident is not the only possible reading. Punctuation does not make text inside parentheses legally inferior, and specific inclusion may show that the general description is too narrow. National Bank requires integrated interpretation, not a license to discard the inconvenient side of an enacted conflict. At least genuine ambiguity remains after ordinary reading, and the Indian-law canon resolves it in favor of the tribes; tribal economic-development purposes support that reading without independently granting immunity.

The dissent would apply state-equivalent treatment to chapter 35's pertinent exemption for a sweepstakes, wagering pool or lottery conducted by a state agency under state law, where the wager is placed with that agency or its authorized employee or agent. Section 4411's occupational tax would then follow its own liability-linked terms. This is a construction of the IGRA cross-reference for covered operations, not an exemption for every tribe, member, game or federal tax. The dissent would reverse and remand for application to each tribe's actual assessments and relief, leaving transaction coverage and calculations to the tax record.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#chickasaw-nation-v-united-states-merits-2001-11-27).

### Correctional Services Corp. v. Malesko — 2001-11-27

**O'Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas, dissenting.** The dissent would reverse and dismiss the corporate constitutional claim. Meyer gives independent force to the distinction between an established officer action and judicial creation of a new entity remedy. A federal custodial duty, useful compensation and the absence of sovereign immunity do not themselves supply that cause. The private defendant weakens the public-fisc concern, but does not eliminate the separate question of who may create a corporate constitutional remedy. Richardson addresses immunity and expressly does not resolve corporate liability or cause creation. Individual actions, state tort remedies, prospective relief and contractual oversight retain their actual scope and reinforce remedial restraint; the dissent does not need to assume that tort relief exactly duplicates every constitutional injury. Limiting the proposed action to corporate policy narrows liability but still adds the disputed defendant. The dissent's objection concerns creation of that cause, not a finding that the company complied with the Constitution or a grant of sovereign status to every contractor.

**Scalia, joined by Thomas, dissenting separately.** They join O'Connor's dissent and add that the Court should not extend implied constitutional damages beyond the recognized Bivens, Davis and Carlson settings even when a proposed extension can be analogized to their reasoning. That distinct objection reaches the Court's claim of a sufficiently close custodial analogy: accepting the analogy does not, in their view, authorize another judicially created damages action. Existing recognized causes remain operative. They do not rely on treating Sandoval as a controlling general abandonment of statutory implication, because its actual statutory holding supplies no such premise. Their separate ground neither decides private employee liability nor overrules the recognized constitutional actions.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#correctional-services-corp-v-malesko-merits-2001-11-27).

### J. E. M. Ag Supply, Inc. v. Pioneer Hi-Bred International, Inc. — 2001-12-10

Scalia concurs and joins the Court in full. Chakrabarty already authoritatively construed the general subject-matter language to reject the supposed ambiguity based on living subject matter. The plant-specific statutes therefore cannot merely be used to clarify that language into the opposite categorical exclusion; a claimed change must satisfy the more demanding inquiry into displacement of the existing rule. This canon explanation supplements rather than narrows the Court's eligibility and coexistence rationale.

Stevens dissents, joined by Breyer, and would treat the plant-specific enactments as the exclusive protection for their covered plant categories. The 1930 Act expressly amended the general patent provision, conditioned protection on asexual reproduction and limited the reproduction right; the later variety statute supplied seed-reproduction protection with saved-seed and research limits. In their view, the utility route bypasses those particular congressional choices, and recodification did not undo them. Different eligibility thresholds do not explain why increased originality should eliminate protections Congress deliberately conferred on agricultural users. Chakrabarty's human-made microorganism lay outside those specialized categories, so the dissent preserves its holding and does not exclude all living inventions or independently qualifying processes. The dissent would reverse on the category defense.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#j-e-m-ag-supply-inc-v-pioneer-hi-bred-international-inc-merits-2001-12-10).

### Schrader v. Blackwell — 2001-12-10

Stevens dissents, joined by Stone-Zsela, Souter and Ginsburg, and would distinguish verified affiliation from the privileges and official representation of recognized-party nomination. Because the candidate has independently qualified and the party actually nominated him, the State must explain why every accurately qualified affiliation cue must be suppressed. Anderson-Burdick requires that fit even when the burden is less than severe; campaign speech does not eliminate the distinct ballot burden. Norman, Tashjian and Eu give weight to the real association and supported standard bearer. Timmons concerned fusion nomination and does not decide this separate informational burden. The dissent would permit the State to verify affiliation and choose accurate wording, and would recognize demonstrated administrative or effective-governing interests rather than impose a universal preferred-label right. It would remand for bounded prospective relief, preserving party-recognition thresholds and leaving the completed election intact.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#schrader-v-blackwell-merits-2001-12-10).

### Dusenbery v. United States — 2002-01-08

Stevens dissents, joined by Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, and agrees that due process does not guarantee receipt. It concludes that the government has not sufficiently demonstrated the final custodial handoff. A confined owner cannot supervise or replace the government's intermediary, so Mullane requires practical attention to how notice reaches that person, including reasonably available safeguards. Evidence of transmission between employees does not adequately establish the final inmate-directed protection. Greene and Mennonite emphasize practical communication; Good and West Covina preserve a meaningful opportunity to respond. The dissent does not infer nonreceipt from the denial or the missing old records, invent a failure rate, demand one particular signature form, or apply later prison practices retroactively. It would set aside the notice judgment and return for the proper consequences of the defect, without treating inadequate notice as proof of ownership or automatic entitlement to the property.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#dusenbery-v-united-states-merits-2002-01-08).

### Great-West Life & Annuity Insurance Co. v. Knudson — 2002-01-08

Souter dissents, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer, and would recognize the asserted return of benefits from a third-party recovery as the traditionally equitable restitution category. The claim is measured by alleged gain rather than the plan-loss compensation rejected in Mertens. A category need have been typically available in equity, not exclusive to equity, and direct possession should not control every restorative theory. Varity supports giving the redress provision practical effect within its limits, while Harris Trust recognizes that appropriate restoration can reach beyond fiduciaries. The government's trust-beneficiary analogy supports that characterization without establishing new property facts. The dissent would remove the categorical remedy bar and return plan interpretation and the actual remedial predicates for adjudication. It would award no reimbursement amount and would not adjudicate absent recipients' property.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#great-west-life-annuity-insurance-co-v-knudson-merits-2002-01-08).

### Toyota Motor Manufacturing, Kentucky, Inc. v. Williams — 2002-01-08

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the occupational-shortcut and mitigation propositions and in setting aside the partial judgment, but rejects a categorical severe-and-permanent threshold. He would assess individualized substantial limitation of important manual functioning through breadth, frequency, duration and practical effect. These are relevant considerations, not separate mandatory elements. Important work-related functions cannot be excluded because they occur at work, and retained household abilities do not automatically defeat coverage. Expected duration informs substantiality without making permanence indispensable. Sutton and Murphy preserve the underlying-impairment inquiry; the difference between working and manual tasks requires correction of the appellate shortcut, not a restriction to only the most pervasive daily-life limitations. His proposed standard still requires proof rather than diagnosis alone, preserves distinct qualification and accommodation issues, and would not grant Toyota automatic judgment. That broader standard is noncontrolling.

These are published noncontrolling positions, with the scope and joins shown in the [public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK1_PUBLIC_LAW.md#toyota-motor-manufacturing-kentucky-inc-v-williams-merits-2002-01-08).

### Kelly v. South Carolina — 2002-01-09

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by no other Justice.** He joins Part I in full. The escape planning and proposed violence while confined invited the inference that the omitted parole information answers. Under Chapman, the complete presentation leaves reasonable doubt whether the misleading sentencing premise contributed to death. Strong aggravation does not resolve that separate inquiry. He would therefore reverse the death-sustaining judgment and require a new lawful penalty proceeding now if the State continues to seek death, preserving guilt. This is a record-specific harmfulness determination, not structural error or an automatic new-hearing rule in every capital case. Simmons and Shafer supply the governing protection and the separate error-effect standard; his disagreement concerns its application and the resulting immediate remedy.

**Justice Scalia, dissenting, joined by no other Justice.** He would affirm against this federal claim because the Constitution does not supply this further parole-information obligation. Gardner's unavailable adverse material and Skipper's excluded rebuttal evidence do not establish a general duty to explain parole law. He accepts Shafer's account of the statutory alternatives without joining the new constitutional application. Treating evidence capable of forecasting danger as sufficient risks erasing Simmons's conditional limit. He resolves neither informational adequacy nor harmlessness and does not join the other dissents.

**Justice Kennedy, dissenting, joined by no other Justice.** He accepts Simmons and Shafer's contextual rule but finds the trigger absent here. In his view the State actually used the material for retribution, character and inability to adapt safely to prison, rather than meaningfully invoking danger from ordinary future release. Parole ineligibility does not answer danger to guards or an escape risk. Evidence capable of supporting a prediction is not necessarily a State invocation of the particular danger the rule addresses. He requires no verbal formula and does not deny that circumstantial evidence can establish the trigger on a different record. He would affirm without separately deciding adequacy or error effect.

**Justice Thomas, dissenting, joined by no other Justice.** Assuming the sentencing alternatives and future-danger predicate, he finds the actual information adequate. Defense argument conveyed permanent imprisonment, the judge directed the ordinary meaning of life, and the jury received neither a direction barring parole consideration nor a response to a parole inquiry that displaced the explanation. The good-time evidence also belongs in the full presentation. As in his Shafer dissent, substance can satisfy Simmons without the requested statutory formula. He therefore would affirm for compliance, not absence of danger or harmlessness. His constitutional disagreement with Simmons remains a reservation, not an independent ground, and he makes no finding about the jurors' actual beliefs.

All four separate positions are noncontrolling at the stated scope. The case returns to South Carolina for the Chapman determination; the federal merits event is complete, while state sentencing proceedings continue under the mandate. No later state result or external consequence is admitted, and no roster or institutional change occurs.

These published positions are noncontrolling at the stated scope. [Public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK2_PUBLIC_LAW.md#kelly-v-south-carolina-merits-2002-01-09).

### Riley v. St. Luke's Episcopal Hospital — 2002-01-09

**Justice Scalia, dissenting, joined by Justice Thomas.** They would reverse on both Article II grounds and bar this nonintervened prosecution while preserving lawful governmental enforcement. A partial financial assignment does not eliminate the sovereign's retained claim, penalties, settlement interests or exposure to a binding judgment. In their view, initiation and prosecution of that public component exercise executive power through a statutory role continuing until the suit ends. The Executive neither selected this prosecutor nor possesses the ordinary chain of direction that execution of the laws requires.

Scalia's Morrison dissent supplies his executive-control premise; Vermont Agency's assignment-based standing opinion expressly reserves this distinct Article II question. His and Thomas's published Laidlaw concern about private public-law enforcement likewise distinguishes an injured claimant from uncontrolled governmental enforcement. Edmond, Hilario and Landry require attention to actual authority, appointment and supervision rather than final judgment power alone. The relator's financial incentive and title do not remove the significant public function. Early qui tam practice and a court-bound assigned claim are substantial contrary considerations, but they do not cure prosecution of the retained public interest without lawful appointment and constitutionally adequate direction.

The statutory safeguards are real but conditional. Later intervention requires good cause, dismissal requires notice and a hearing opportunity, settlement has its separate judicial standard, and discovery protection depends on its stated showing. These measures do not supply the missing selection and direction. The dissent does not demand presidential at-will removal of every inferior officer or decide every private statutory action. It would return for the procedural consequence of disallowing Riley's nonintervened prosecution, including any lawful governmental election, without invalidating section 3730(a), dismissing government claims with prejudice, presuming intervention or appointment, or treating the entire Act as inseverable.

These are the complete material noncontrolling positions of Scalia and Thomas in their common dissent. The federal merits event is complete; the Fifth Circuit's remand remains operative. No liability finding, subsequent governmental election, external consequence or institutional change is admitted.

These published positions are noncontrolling at the stated scope. [Public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK2_PUBLIC_LAW.md#riley-v-st-lukes-episcopal-hospital-merits-2002-01-09).

### EEOC v. Waffle House, Inc. — 2002-01-15

**Justice Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Justice Scalia.** They join Part I because Circuit City's governing employment exclusion removes the FAA as a source of enforcement, despite their continuing disagreement with that precedent. They also join Part III's ordinary liability, remedy and nonduplication conditions. The Commission may bring its independent public action and cannot itself be compelled to arbitrate.

They reject Parts II and IV because nonparty assent does not answer whether a court should grant relief for Baker's individual injury in disregard of his own enforceable commitment. Section 706(g)'s requirement of appropriate relief and the distinction between permission to sue and entitlement to a particular remedy, in their view, require respect for a valid beneficiary promise even though broader public relief remains available. First Options addresses actual assent to arbitration, not that distinct remedial question; Great-West similarly preserves attention to the statute authorizing the particular remedy.

The FAA cannot establish enforceability here. They would therefore vacate its categorical restriction and remand to consider any properly available independent state-law ground, the promise's actual scope, and its consequence for individual relief under their view. They presume neither a valid independent ground nor a completed arbitration, and ordinary preservation limits remain. Their position does not compel arbitration of the agency or affirm the existing restriction on a displaced FAA premise.

These published positions are noncontrolling at the stated scope. [Public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK2_PUBLIC_LAW.md#eeoc-v-waffle-house-inc-merits-2002-01-15).

### United States v. Arvizu — 2002-01-15

**Justice Scalia, concurring, joined by no other Justice.** He joins the Court's full application of governing law and its reversal and bounded remand. He retains his Ornelas dissent's preference for deferential review and his objection that giving weight to an inferential conclusion can be conceptually inconsistent with independently deciding the ultimate issue. A factual description of the children's waving can receive factual protection; whether it is suspicious cannot be sheltered from constitutional judgment simply by calling it an officer inference. Reversal follows here even under traditional independent review. His join applies Ornelas as governing law without endorsing its original choice of standard, dispensing with innocent explanations or authorizing the subsequent search.

These published positions are noncontrolling at the stated scope. [Public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK2_PUBLIC_LAW.md#united-states-v-arvizu-merits-2002-01-15).

### National Cable & Telecommunications Assn., Inc. v. Gulf Power Co. — 2002-01-16

Thomas, joined by Souter, concurs in the wireless holding but would vacate the mixed-service portion for a reasoned agency determination. If cable-modem access is cable service, subsection (d)'s formula controls; if it is telecommunications service, subsection (e)'s formula controls. Only after resolving those possibilities can residual authority under subsection (b) support the particular regulatory choice. The service supplied by an Internet service provider and the transmission carrying customers to that provider are distinct classification questions. The Commission's treatment of one does not necessarily resolve the other. Permian Basin's demand for a reasoned rate method supports returning that choice to the agency. Probable ultimate jurisdiction and policy favoring broadband deployment cannot supply the missing statutory explanation. This position rejects categorical noncoverage but withholds approval of the existing unexplained agency determination.

These published positions are noncontrolling at the stated scope. [Public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK2_PUBLIC_LAW.md#national-cable-telecommunications-assn-inc-v-gulf-power-co-merits-2002-01-16).

### Kansas v. Crane — 2002-01-22

Scalia, joined by Thomas, agrees that due process does not require complete inability to control conduct. He would hold that a genuine present mental abnormality or disorder causally making repeated sexual violence likely supplies the substantive limitation, when proved under Kansas's burden. An additional independent control finding is not constitutionally required. The new serious-difficulty formulation, in his view, gives uncertain guidance and turns descriptions of an individual's condition into a universal extra element. This position neither treats a label or danger alone as sufficient nor allows a high burden to create a missing substantive predicate. It preserves Foucha's ended-predicate rule and Hendricks and Seling's separate review of actual punitive operation. He would reverse the complete-incapacity rule and remand under the statute's genuine condition-and-causal-danger requirements.

These published positions are noncontrolling at the stated scope. [Public opinion account](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK2_PUBLIC_LAW.md#kansas-v-crane-merits-2002-01-22).

### Lee v. Kemna — 2002-01-22

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas, would affirm because Rule 24.10 retained a substantive function apart from the written-motion form.
