No separate opinion is filed. The exact partial join is stated in Opinion Topology and the authority for Part V.

### Thompson v. Western States Medical Center — 2002-04-29

[Record](../records/thompson_v_western_states_medical_center_merits_2002-04-29.md)

**Thomas, concurring.** He joins the full fit holding because these restrictions fail even the lesser Central Hudson test. He separately regards suppression of accurate product information to prevent its effect on willing listeners as the mechanism his 44 Liquormart position rejects. Drug safety is legitimate, but that objective does not itself justify withholding accurate information; direct drug controls remain distinct. His concurrence is not judgment-only agreement and does not supply the Court with a broader categorical rule.

**Breyer, dissenting in part, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg.** The dissent would reverse the constitutional invalidation insofar as it rests on promotion to consumers. The statutory balance preserves medically needed preparations while preventing cumulative risks from a growing market of products that have not undergone approval. Product promotion can generate demand apart from a clinician's prior individualized assessment. Production caps, wholesale restrictions and controls on equipment or advance preparation may either exclude needed treatment or fail to address that demand. General service advertising and continuing physician involvement preserve meaningful channels. Under ordinary reasonable-fit review, the dissent therefore finds the consumer restriction proportionate despite its burden on truthful information. It reserves the distinct question of physician-directed promotion because that issue requires separate consideration; it neither sustains every communication restriction nor announces a benefit-condition exception to the First Amendment.

### US Airways, Inc. v. Barnett — 2002-04-29

[Record](../records/us_airways_v_barnett_merits_2002-04-29.md)

**Chief Justice Stone-Zsela, concurring.** He joins the Court's opinion in full and emphasizes that the special-circumstances showing must be a real and practical opportunity. Evidence that the employer retains power to change the system, that exceptions are already frequent, or that one more exception is unlikely to matter can carry the employee's showing in the particular case. Those avenues must receive substantive consideration rather than being treated as a formality. His full join preserves the Court's requirement to explain the actual effect on seniority expectations; retained power alone is not an automatic entitlement, and the employer's separate hardship defense remains.

**Justice Stevens, concurring.** He joins the full seniority presumption and remand. Seniority bears substantially on reasonableness, not only on hardship, but the employer's categorical position still fails. The existing interactive-process ground remains outside this seniority decision. Material application questions include why the mailroom position became open for bidding, whether the request is best understood as reassignment or continuation of the existing placement, and its actual effect on other employees. Barnett must answer the pertinent questions to establish special circumstances; the concurrence treats them as record-dependent inquiries, not a new mandatory checklist or findings in his favor.

**Justice O'Connor, concurring.** She joins the Court's opinion in full while explaining her preferred legal-entitlement rule. A position is unavailable when another employee occupies it or has an enforceable entitlement to it; an expectation under a noncontractual seniority policy alone supplies no legal possessor. On that approach, this policy does not defeat Barnett's initial reasonable-accommodation showing, and US Airways would have to establish actual hardship. She objects that a particularized special-circumstances inquiry can blur the distinction between general reasonableness and the employer's particular hardship burden. She nevertheless adopts the Court's statutory rule because a common rule is necessary and the practical exception will often produce the same result as her preferred approach. Disclaimers, reserved authority and actual exceptions can weaken expectations; her concurrence does not find that every noncontractual system does so. Her full join includes the employee's meaningful opportunity and the separate employer defense.

**Justice Scalia, dissenting, joined by Justice Thomas.** They would reverse the seniority ruling and return for a disposition rejecting the demanded priority. Reading sections 12112(a) and (b)(5)(A) together, they require reasonable removal of workplace obstacles that disability prevents an employee from overcoming. A standing requirement or inaccessible workstation can be such an obstacle even though expressed neutrally. A bona fide seniority allocation unrelated to disability is different: an exception grants priority over coworkers rather than removing the disability-created barrier. Reassignment still requires the employer to seek and offer a suitable vacant job within the lawful allocation instead of simply dismissing a worker unable to perform the former job. Their Martin and Olmstead positions support that distinction at their respective statutory scopes, without supplying a substitute Title I rule. They reject the Court's special-circumstances exception as both beyond the statutory duty and uncertain even for established systems. Their ground is not immunity for every neutral policy, and their proposed disposition preserves independent unreviewed claims.

**Justice Souter, dissenting, joined by Justice Ginsburg.** They would affirm the rejection of employer summary judgment because Barnett has met the initial reasonableness showing, leaving US Airways to prove actual hardship. The ADA omits the express seniority protections found in Title VII and the ADEA; the committee reports likewise treat seniority as a consideration rather than a special exemption. Hardison's different statute and protected collective agreement do not justify a presumption for this employer's unilateral policy. They give reasonableness independent work through feasibility, suitability and the actual change requested. Barnett seeks to retain a job he performs, displaces no incumbent, and confronts a policy the employer disclaims as contractual and reserves power to change. Those circumstances satisfy the initial showing without establishing ultimate liability or eliminating evidence of operational disruption. They preserve the distinct federal force of enforceable labor contracts recognized in Lincoln Mills and Charles Dowd Box; no such agreement governs this request. The employer's proof of actual significant difficulty or expense, including concrete noncash effects, remains necessary under their view.

### City of Los Angeles v. Alameda Books, Inc. — 2002-05-13

[Record](../records/city_of_los_angeles_v_alameda_books_merits_2002-05-13.md)

**Kennedy, concurring and joining the Court's opinion in full.** He accepts the initial inference only within a genuine dispersal theory. The ordinance must reduce harmful conditions while leaving the quantity and accessibility of protected expression substantially intact. Reducing speech or its audience, and thereby reducing associated harms, is not a permissible secondary-effects mechanism. Renton's land-use category and Turner's actual-operation inquiry permit a supported concentration inference, but the economic premise that the ordinance disperses rather than suppresses remains open to meaningful challenge. His illustrations supply no finding about patron numbers, successful relocation or business survival. This distinct necessary condition is not an additional majority holding.

**Souter, dissenting, Part II, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer.** Even assuming the genuine secondary-effects framework, the study fails to connect isolated combined operations with the harm caused by the concentration of separate establishments. Counting the bookstore and arcade as separate legal categories does not show more patrons or criminal opportunity in these small undivided premises. Compelling their separation may double operating costs and curtail expression without dispersing any demonstrated harm. That is an objection to the City's inferential bridge, not a finding that closure will occur. Renton permits relevant outside studies and ordinary experience, but the support must address the change being regulated; no perfect study is demanded. The dissent would preserve the businesses' declaration and injunction on this sufficient evidentiary ground.

**Souter, dissenting, Part I, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg only.** Content-correlated regulation requires independent attention to actual effects and evidentiary safeguards against disguised suppression. O'Brien's nonsuppression premise and Erie's targeting rule prevent the selected adult-content category from receiving automatic deference. The separate evidence ground suffices for affirmance without a final classification ruling. Breyer does not join this broader account.

### Henderson v. Norris — 2002-05-13

[Record](../records/henderson_v_norris_merits_2002-05-13.md)

**Kennedy, concurring in Part I and dissenting from Parts II–III, joined by O'Connor throughout the separate writing and by Thomas in the assumed-framework application only.** The dissent would restore denial of the sentencing claim. The tiny quantity, first conviction and lack of ordinary parole make the sentence severe, but the completed distribution offense and supported record do not meet the extraordinarily demanding threshold under Harmelin. Legislative judgments can account for distribution's preventive and social harms without measuring sentence length arithmetically by weight. The comparison evidence does not convert the exceptional severity into a constitutional prohibition. The dissent acknowledges the real difference from Harmelin's quantity and treats clemency as discretionary grace; it assumes neither a large enterprise nor prior convictions. Thomas's agreement assumes narrow proportionality for this disposition and does not adopt its constitutional foundation. Merits denial makes a separately unestablished new-rule bar unnecessary to these positions.

**Scalia, concurring in Part I and dissenting from Parts II–III.** Harmelin's distinct textual and historical account rejects prison-duration proportionality as an independent requirement of the Cruel and Unusual Punishments Clause. The actual sentence and comparisons may present a sentencing-policy objection, but they do not establish the asserted constitutional right on his ground. His acceptance of the Excessive Fines inquiry in Bajakajian supplies no prison-duration rule. He accepts the controlling temporal law and does not rely on amended section 2254(d) or a fictitious criminal history. His rationale is distinct from the other dissenters' conclusion that this sentence passes a narrow proportionality test.

### Ashcroft v. American Civil Liberties Union; No. 00-1293; 535 U.S. 564. — 2002-05-13

**O'Connor, concurring.** She joins the confined holding because the present showing does not identify sufficient otherwise protected material made unlawful solely through community variation once prurience and serious value are considered. Pope's objective-value inquiry and Sable's distinction between an adult ban and confined protection matter, but the Web's inability to select recipients prevents treating conventional mail and telephone distribution as conclusive. She affirmatively regards a national standard as constitutionally permissible and necessary for reasonable Internet obscenity regulation. Her judgment neither forecloses an adequately supported facial challenge nor denies later as-applied relief.

**Thomas, concurring, joined by Scalia.** The prurience and objective serious-value requirements materially narrow the relevant category compared with the CDA. Miller, Pope, Hamling and Sable support rejecting community variation as a sufficient objection to this statutory category; the existing showing does not establish substantial variation in the covered material. This broader explanation would reject the community-standards objection more fully than the Court's common ground. It does not adopt a national-standard amendment, resolve COPA's other constitutional defects, or treat statutory commercial purpose as permission to suppress protected adult literature. They join continued interim protection while the other injunction grounds are considered.

**Souter, concurring, joined by Kennedy and Ginsburg.** The extent of protected expression cannot be measured until the statute's reach is determined. Commercial coverage, the relevant whole work or communication, the different ages within the under-seventeen category and venue consequences affect the relationship between legitimate regulation and protected expression. Reno, Playboy and Free Speech Coalition require attention to actual adult access, not an assumption that value for minors cures the adult burden. These Justices expressly preserve the community-standards challenge as part of the integrated inquiry: after construction, geographic variation may still prove decisive. Their join does not approve local standards as constitutionally harmless, depend on hypothetical geographic controls, or adopt a national standard.

**Breyer, concurring in part and in the judgment.** He joins the statutory background and disposition but reads community standards to mean the national adult community. The ordinary phrase in context, its legislative explanation and avoidance of serious constitutional difficulty support that construction in his view. A local standard would give restrictive communities practical control over protected speech distributed nationally. Variation among juries applying one national standard is different from variation among the governing substantive standards themselves. This construction is necessary to his agreement, not a merely optional possibility. It does not resolve the other COPA objections, and he agrees that the injunction should continue.

**Stevens, dissenting.** He would affirm the preliminary injunction on the community-standards ground. His objections in Smith and Sable, together with Reno's adult-access rule, apply to the established inability of national Web speakers to avoid restrictive local audiences. Serious value for minors does not define all expression adults may constitutionally receive. Narrowing the commercial category or the whole-work unit may reduce absolute coverage without curing substantial overbreadth relative to legitimate applications. Verification burdens do not erase that injury. He rejects insertion of a national standard into language with an established community-based meaning. His position preserves the legitimate interest in protecting children and ordinary restrictions on unprotected material; it does not require invalidating every future Internet safeguard.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#ashcroft-v-aclu-merits-2002-05-13).

### Lapides v. Board of Regents of University System of Georgia; No. 01-298; 535 U.S. 613. — 2002-05-13

No separate writing is filed.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#lapides-v-board-of-regents-merits-2002-05-13).

### Verizon Communications Inc. v. FCC; Nos. 00-511, 00-555, 00-587, 00-590 and 00-602; 535 U.S. 467. — 2002-05-13

**Breyer, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He accepts forward-looking cost, rejects mandatory recovery of embedded investment and the present confiscation theory, and joins the complete bounded combination holding. His disagreement concerns the adequacy of the explanation for the efficient-network pricing assumption. A compulsory leasing price based on best available technology and lowest-cost configuration can discourage replacement investment and independent facilities construction while the incumbent continues to operate existing assets. State Farm's demand for a reasoned connection between means and statutory ends requires a more convincing account of how this particular benchmark advances durable competition and deregulation. Administrative latitude and a prohibition on traditional rate-based proceedings do not alone answer that concern. He would retain the partial vacatur and return the methodology to the agency, without prescribing an embedded-cost formula or repayment of all sunk costs. Necessary, compensated connection assistance poses a different issue: reasonable-ability limits, genuine network defenses and recoverable connection costs answer the concern about unnecessary incumbent assembly. His agreement on combining does not supply agreement on TELRIC.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#verizon-communications-v-fcc-merits-2002-05-13).

### Voting Integrity Project, Inc. v. Keisling; reviewed judgment 259 F.3d 1169 (9th Cir. 2001); no Supreme Court docket supplied. — 2002-05-13

No separate writing is filed.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#voting-integrity-project-v-keisling-merits-2002-05-13).

### Alabama v. Shelton; No. 00-1214; 535 U.S. 654. — 2002-05-20

Thomas's dissent, joined by Scalia and Kennedy, would reverse only the present invalidation of the suspended jail term. It reads Argersinger and Scott to make actual loss of liberty, rather than a presently unexecuted threat, the constitutional attachment point. Shelton has not been imprisoned; a violation, discretionary revocation and a determination that other sanctions are inadequate would precede incarceration. The dissent would address the adequacy of execution procedures when that issue is presented.

The dissent accepts that competent self-representation is not an informed waiver. It does not approve ordinary revocation as a substitute for a counseled trial. A complete counseled retrial, or possibly other adequate safeguards, could protect against unconstitutional execution, and no such procedure is found to exist here. Nichols's defined fine-only Guidelines permission does not authorize unconditional later imprisonment for this offense. The dissent also questions the asserted equivalence between consensual pretrial diversion and post-trial probation and the inference that existing state appointment practices establish the costs of counsel for every suspended sentence. Practical convenience does not decide the Constitution, but uncertain cost comparisons do not justify moving Scott's boundary. The dissent preserves the conviction and independent monetary punishment and leaves freestanding probation and the late contempt theory undecided.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#alabama-v-shelton-merits-2002-05-20).

### Brown v. Gilmore; reviewed judgment 258 F.3d 265 (4th Cir. 2001); no Supreme Court docket supplied. — 2002-05-20

No separate writing.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#brown-v-gilmore-merits-2002-05-20).

### Mathias v. WorldCom Technologies, Inc.; No. 00-878; 535 U.S. 682. — 2002-05-20

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the dismissal. Applying New York Telephone Co. v. Maltbie, he treats this request as an effort to revise unnecessary adverse reasoning in an otherwise favorable judgment. The commissioners' own regulatory order was sustained; in his view, the challenged jurisdictional and immunity reasoning establishes no independently binding adverse consequence requiring correction. The binding favorable merits and the separately denied motions do not supply the different adverse component contemplated by his rule. His conclusion concerns the legal effect of this judgment, not an inference that the motions were never made or denied.

He would not review a favorable judgment solely to edit such nonbinding adverse reasoning. A genuine adverse judgment component or independently binding collateral effect presents a materially different question. He adopts no categorical Article III prohibition on all prevailing-party appeals, reaches none of the three substantive questions and leaves the lower judgment undisturbed without endorsing its unnecessary findings.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#mathias-v-worldcom-technologies-dig-2002-05-20).

### United States v. Cotton; No. 01-687; 535 U.S. 625. — 2002-05-20

Scalia concurs in part and in the judgment. He joins Parts I, II-A and III, including the genuine constitutional error, forfeiture, assumed substantial-rights injury and denial of correction under the independent fourth condition. He assumes that the error is plain without joining Part II-B's affirmative intervening-law plainness rule. Because the fourth condition is unsatisfied on this record even on that assumption, the broader plainness determination is unnecessary to his vote. He does not treat appellate assessment of the evidence as a jury verdict, find an intentional waiver or adopt a categorical exception to Apprendi.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#united-states-v-cotton-merits-2002-05-20).

### Verizon Maryland Inc. v. Public Service Commission of Maryland; United States v. Public Service Commission of Maryland; Nos. 00-1531 and 00-1711; 535 U.S. 635. — 2002-05-20

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in Parts I–III and dissents from the form of the judgment. He would reverse and remand because §1331 and the prospective officer-relief route permit the claim against the responsible commissioners to proceed. He agrees that federal jurisdiction, statutory authority, consent and remedy do separate work; participation does not establish constructive consent, and intercarrier financial consequences do not make the requested future-enforcement restraint a State-damages action. His reversal would reinstate adjudication of the federal claim, leaving federal compliance and the precise injunction for decision below. He would not determine direct Commission waiver or the compensation merits. His disagreement concerns reversal rather than vacatur, not the controlling threshold rules or the requirement of further adjudication.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#verizon-maryland-v-public-service-commission-merits-2002-05-20).

### Bell v. Cone; No. 01-400; 535 U.S. 685. — 2002-05-28

Justice Stevens joins the correct statutory and prejudice framework but dissents from the applications and judgment. He would affirm the Cronic-based sentence-only conditional writ. In his view, the combined failure to present an individualized case for life and answer the prosecution's request for death eliminated the relevant capital-sentencing function. He acknowledges the mercy opening, references to existing experts, favorable military cross-examination and protective objections; he considers them insufficient to constitute meaningful adversarial testing in the proceeding as a whole. His rule is not that every omitted witness or waived closing is structural, and he makes no finding that later illness establishes trial incapacity.

If Cronic is rejected, Stevens would also leave the ordinary tactical claim open on remand. He would require examination of the asserted legal misunderstandings, the performance ground's independence from the erroneous prejudice inquiry and its relationship to the disputed restrictions on mitigation development. Pritchett's limited support for expecting appellate correction does not, in his view, excuse surrender of a meaningful penalty defense. He accepts the State's independent-ground argument as substantial but would not foreclose ordinary appellate review on this record. He finds neither Strickland deficiency nor actual prejudice as an alternative present holding. His primary remedy preserves the existing 180-day structure for a new lawful penalty proceeding; the convictions and unused alternative sentence grounds are unaffected.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#Bell-v-Cone-Merits-2002-05-28).

### Federal Maritime Commission v. South Carolina State Ports Authority; No. 01-46; 535 U.S. 743. — 2002-05-28

Justice Kennedy, joined by O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas, dissents from the agency-boundary holding and reversal. The four would vacate and remand. Compulsory pleadings, response, discovery, hearing and adjudication of personal liability, in their view, impose the private-suit burden from which immunity protects a State. Commission supervision, an executive label and lack of immediate execution do not remove that burden or the eventual order's evidentiary consequences. They would not distinguish the cease-and-desist request automatically from reparations merely by calling it prospective; no officer defendant is created by the complaint.

They nevertheless reject the Fourth Circuit's complete dismissal rationale because the governing Florida Prepaid and Garrett rule permits unmistakably authorized State exposure under valid Article I power. They retain their original structural objection while applying that controlling rule. Their remand would identify the operative enactment, the particular complaint and remedy, and the statutory authorization before final disposition. Public-terminal coverage, the word person and commerce power alone neither establish nor defeat the necessary clarity; no single required verbal formula or present abrogation answer follows. California did not decide this private-immunity question. Actual government investigation and enforcement, proper connected-officer relief and later collection remain separate. Their proposed remand removes the dismissal direction but does not yet authorize ordinary merits adjudication on the Court's ground.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#Federal-Maritime-Commission-v-South-Carolina-State-Ports-Authority-Merits-2002-05-28).

### Festo Corp. v. Shoketsu Kinzoku Kogyo Kabushiki Co.; No. 00-1543; 535 U.S. 722. — 2002-05-28



Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#Festo-Corp-v-Shoketsu-Kinzoku-Kogyo-Kabushiki-Co-Merits-2002-05-28).

### Gisbrecht v. Barnhart; No. 01-131; 535 U.S. 789. — 2002-05-28

Justice Scalia dissents from Parts I–II and the judgment, while joining the independently stated judicial-review and statutory safeguards. He would value reasonable hours at a reasonable market rate, without a nonpayment-risk multiplier, and affirm these three awards. Section 406(b)'s direction to determine a reasonable fee, in his view, calls for valuation of services rather than enforcement of the contingent percentage. Changing the payor does not change that judicial task, and the percentage maximum remains useful as an additional ceiling. Dague supports this approach without already deciding this statute. He rejects substituting EAJA's limited rate for the relevant market rate.

He objects that the Court combines an agreement made before the result with an after-the-result reasonableness review whose reductions remain less definite than lodestar. Earnings, dependents and delay can increase benefits without corresponding work; attorney-caused delay can also violate the bargain's implicit obligation. The supplied local rates and hours, rejection of risk enhancement and explanations of Miller's objections support affirmance under his method. He accepts independent substantiation, the ceiling and the overlapping-fee refund, without joining the Court's agreement method or finding misconduct.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#Gisbrecht-v-Barnhart-Merits-2002-05-28).

### In re Prudential Insurance Co. of America Sales Practice Litigation; review of 261 F.3d 355 (3d Cir. 2001). No Supreme Court docket supplied. — 2002-05-28



Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#In-re-Prudential-Insurance-Co-of-America-Sales-Practice-Litigation-Merits-2002-05-28).

### South Camden Citizens in Action v. New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection; review of 274 F.3d 771 (3d Cir. 2001), lower-court Nos. 01-2224 and 01-2296. No Supreme Court docket supplied. — 2002-05-28

Justice Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer, concurs in the direct-route remand and dissents from Part I. He would reconsider Powell's §1983 holding to the extent it rejects this identified entitlement. The statutory beneficiaries, nondiscrimination protection, express implementation authority and a valid, sufficiently definite implementing requirement can, in his view, define the individual protection that §1983 enforces. Blessing's particular-right inquiry and Wright's and Wilder's recognition of authorized elaboration support that conclusion. He does not treat every regulation or administrative command as a right or establish a general entitlement to equal environmental burdens. Sandoval independently requires consideration of the direct claim, so his §1983 disagreement does not undermine remand, actual causation, proper-defendant or equitable requirements.

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs in the §1983 rejection and direct prospective adjudication but dissents from vacating the appellate judgment in full. He would affirm the rejected §1983 basis and vacate only the foreclosure of the retained Title VI theory. The two channels answer different questions, and Sandoval's direct-action rule leaves Powell's §1983 rule intact. He would require prompt determination of the covered recipient, actual regulatory duty, causal effect, proper prospective defendant and interim-relief requirements. Protection requires a supported lawful order; his disposition would not automatically restore the injunction or decide the permit's validity.

Opinion topology and the controlling/noncontrolling boundary: [exact public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK5_PUBLIC_LAW.md#South-Camden-Citizens-in-Action-v-New-Jersey-Department-of-Environmental-Protection-Merits-2002-05-28).

### Holmes Group, Inc. v. Vornado Air Circulation Systems, Inc. — 2002-06-03

Ginsburg, joined by O'Connor, concurs in Part II and in the judgment. She would allow the Federal Circuit to review a compulsory patent counterclaim actually adjudicated by the district court, together with the other determinations in that case. A counterclaim is an independent affirmative patent claim, and the appellate allocation differs from the plaintiff's original choice of trial forum. Cardinal Chemical's independent-controversy principle and the statutory purpose of uniform appellate patent adjudication support that distinction in her view. Here the Rule 54(b) judgment adjudicated only the trade-dress claim, while the patent counterclaim remained stayed. The Federal Circuit therefore lacked the asserted jurisdiction even under her approach. She joins the definite transfer and its statutory conditions; her alternative jurisdictional rule is noncontrolling.

Published authors and joins: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK6_PUBLIC_LAW.md#holmes-group-inc-v-vornado-air-circulation-systems-inc-merits-2002-06-03).

### Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Echazabal — 2002-06-10

Stone-Zsela concurs in Parts II and III and in the judgment. He reads §12113(a)'s general qualification defense, its accommodation condition and subsection (b)'s nonexclusive example as independently authorizing the bounded own-health defense. The EEOC has actual rulemaking authority, and its significant-risk, objective-evidence and accommodation safeguards receive substantial persuasive weight because they implement the statutory limits. His conclusion does not depend on treating the agency's choice as independently controlling. Bragdon's objective method and the separation of coverage from qualification prevent a generalized paternalistic veto; Chevron must prove the actual conditions on the preserved medical record.

Published authors and joins: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK6_PUBLIC_LAW.md#chevron-usa-inc-v-echazabal-merits-2002-06-10).

### Devlin v. Scardelletti — 2002-06-10

Thomas, joined by Scalia and Kennedy, dissents. A mandatory class may bind its members through a representative without making each member an independent litigant. Felzen distinguishes participation in settlement supervision from authority to continue litigation, and Marino and Rule 24 supply the ordinary route to personal party status. Devlin's own pension interest and inability to opt out establish the force of the judgment, but in their view do not eliminate that procedural requirement. The right to challenge an actual intervention denial supplies a separate avenue; it does not undo the denial already affirmed here. They would affirm the status dismissal, without deciding settlement fairness, adequate representation or the pension merits, and without adopting a universal prohibition on every nonparty appeal.

Published authors and joins: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK6_PUBLIC_LAW.md#devlin-v-scardelletti-merits-2002-06-10).

### McKune v. Lile — 2002-06-10

O'Connor dissents, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. All four recognize real incriminating exposure and reject immunity inferred from confidentiality or nonprosecution. They would reverse the Fifth Amendment injunction because these actual restrictions, within an unchanged lawful sentence and without lost credits or proved particular danger, do not exert sufficiently severe pressure. They accept that existing conditions are taken away automatically; automatic linkage proves the connection to silence but does not itself prove substantial compulsion. Garrity and the employment, licensing and contracting cases involve materially different consequences. Woodard and Baxter preserve the distinction between a difficult choice and compelled testimony without directly authorizing every automatic sanction. O'Connor rejects an exclusive atypical-and-significant-hardship prerequisite, a freestanding-entitlement prerequisite, or a rule making sentence lengthening necessary. Criminal-process consequences cannot simply supply a universal magnitude threshold for extraprocess efforts to obtain testimony. Her rule leaves serious penalties, compelled waivers, sentence extensions and criminal use of compelled answers unresolved.

Kennedy also dissents, joined by Scalia and Thomas, adopting the common actual-severity ground and adding the importance of ordinary custody and rehabilitative incentives. Lawful imprisonment supplies the context for assessing these conditions; every prison benefit cannot be equated with outside employment or livelihood. Their Sandin dissent informs that position but does not govern the Fifth Amendment inquiry or displace current Sandin law. Kennedy's Mitchell no-inference join addressed proof of initial criminal punishment, unlike this unchanged sentence; Scalia's and Thomas's Mitchell positions distinguish compulsion from noncompulsory incentives. Their Woodard joins preserve actual compulsion as a separate limit. They do not find inevitable safety equivalence, sole effective treatment, fictional immunity, or categorical permission for every ordinary confinement sanction. O'Connor does not join this additional writing.

Published authors and joins: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK6_PUBLIC_LAW.md#mckune-v-lile-merits-2002-06-10).

### National Railroad Passenger Corp. v. Morgan — 2002-06-10

O'Connor concurs in part and dissents in part. In Part I of her separate opinion, joined by Breyer alone, she expresses the view that a notice or reasonable-discovery rule should apply to discrete acts, while recognizing that this known-discrimination record does not require deciding its operation. That observation creates no controlling discovery exception. Scalia and Kennedy do not join it.

In Part II, joined by Scalia and Kennedy, she would limit hostile-environment liability to occurrences within the charge window while permitting older conduct to establish the character, severity or discriminatory cause of timely conditions. Their position limits liability for the older occurrences; exclusion of compensation follows from that limit rather than from a damages-only cap. They accept harassment as a collective wrong and do not demand that each incident independently meet the severe-or-pervasive standard. Harris's totality inquiry does not itself erase the deadline once a known actionable injury is complete. They reject Amtrak's suggestion that early knowledge extinguishes a later new violation, but would reverse the broader environment revival as well as discrete revival. Preserved evidentiary and instructional objections still require appellate consideration; they do not seek automatic restoration of the defense verdict. Breyer does not join Part II.

Published authors and joins: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK6_PUBLIC_LAW.md#national-railroad-passenger-corp-v-morgan-merits-2002-06-10).

### United States v. Farmer — 2002-06-10

**Scalia, concurring in part and in the judgment.** Justice Scalia joins Parts I and III: immediate review, the supported hearing threshold and its application, the defendant's confined preponderance burden, and the conditional release consequence. He does not join Part II's Mathews rationale. His sufficient ground is the distinction between preliminary seizure and the meaningful later adversary opportunity in this continuing civil-seizure setting. His Good position accepted the sufficiency of preliminary procedures on that different record; it did not authorize a warrant to eliminate the later contest altogether. Monsanto left this hearing question open. The prolonged civil custody, particular claim of legitimate property, immediate trial-counsel need and government's conditional acceptance of a hearing support the focused opportunity here. He rejects an open-ended balancing license to redesign all criminal restraint procedures; he agrees that the hearing neither decides the indictment nor authorizes spending proved proceeds.

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** The Chief Justice joins Parts I and II and the order requiring a prompt hearing. Farmer's specific sworn showing meets the threshold; the time-sensitive defense interest, concrete risk of error and value of bounded testing satisfy Mathews. He agrees that Farmer must establish actual need for the identified funds by a preponderance. He dissents from Part III because, once taint is materially contested, he would require the government to justify continued restraint through the applicable probable-cause connection to forfeiture or another independently valid basis. Probable cause of an offense does not establish the taint of every asset. In his view, requiring the owner to disprove the government's case through inaccessible information improperly allocates that part of the hearing. He would affirm the hearing entitlement with modified instructions and require release of the needed amount lacking that support. He does not claim access to proved forfeitable proceeds or extend this counsel-focused appeal to living expenses, interest or final forfeiture merits. His allocation is noncontrolling.

Published authors and joins: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK6_PUBLIC_LAW.md#united-states-v-farmer-merits-2002-06-10).

### Barnes v. Gorman — 2002-06-17

Stevens concurs in the judgment, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer. The commissioners are sued in official capacities, so the punitive award falls on the municipal entity. Newport's established municipal-punishment limitation excludes that award absent clear congressional displacement, which the incorporated disability remedies do not supply. Compensation for the plaintiff's loss remains distinct from punishment borne by the public. This ground neither grants immunity to every individual wrongdoer nor reopens the separate ruling that the Police Board is not an arm of the State.

The three expressly acknowledge that Newport was neither raised nor decided below. They nevertheless reach this existing defendant-specific legal limit within the presented punitive-remedy question because official-capacity status is established and no new evidentiary finding or retrial is needed, avoiding the broader question of private-recipient remedies. Newport's own treatment of a defaulted question arose in different circumstances, including lower-court consideration of its merits, and does not automatically excuse this nonpreservation. Stevens's earlier procedural dissent in Newport remains a caution about default, not a reason to misdescribe the ground as preserved. This confined choice creates no general exception to preservation.

They withhold the Court's general statutory exclusion. Franklin's appropriate-remedy principle, the distinct recipient responsibility discussed in Davis, and their objections to added remedy restrictions in Kolstad and Great-West counsel against using a funding analogy to decide the entire range of private-recipient relief. They need not adopt a general affirmative punitive-availability rule to resolve this municipal award. They therefore support removal of this punitive component, preserve compensation and seek no punitive-culpability or amount-review remand.

Published authors and joins: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK6_PUBLIC_LAW.md#barnes-v-gorman-merits-2002-06-17).

### Watchtower Bible & Tract Society of New York, Inc. v. Village of Stratton — 2002-06-17

Thomas concurs in the judgment. His McIntyre concurrence grounds anonymous advocacy in the historical protection of anonymous pamphleteering; his Buckley concurrence applies strict scrutiny to direct burdens on speech. The advance personal registration and permission required here directly burden that protected activity and are not narrowly fitted to the asserted interests. Actual fraud, trespass and resident-controlled exclusion provide materially more targeted means than licensing all peaceful religious and political advocacy. He also agrees that the ordinance fails the Court's less demanding fit inquiry, but that alternative conclusion does not replace his strict-scrutiny framework or make him a full joiner in the Court's method. His Thomas v. Chicago Park District join preserves genuinely fitted shared-space administration. The result rests on the objective speech burden, not a speaker's subjective religious or patriotic refusal to comply, and creates no independent free-exercise holding or immunity from lawful owner exclusion.

Published authors and joins: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK6_PUBLIC_LAW.md#watchtower-bible-tract-society-v-village-of-stratton-merits-2002-06-17).

### Balderas v. Texas — 2002-06-17

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in the judgment.** Part I maintains Valdespino's two capacity routes and the surviving statutory conditions. Rigorous proof means reliable proof of dependability under the ordinary preponderance burden, not a higher burden, compulsory study, fixed winning percentage or permanent coalition. A single group's outside electoral support differs from aggregation of separate protected groups.

Part II finds the appeal's majority-registration, compactness and findings objections insufficient to require vacatur. Appellants offered election-performance evidence, including reaggregated contests and cross-examination; their case was not devoid of functional proof. But they sought seven registered-voter-majority districts and argued that those proposals exceeded the existing majority threshold. Evidence offered to prove those districts effective does not itself establish that a preserved claim was rejected solely because a protected group lacked a numerical majority. No particular terminology is required to preserve an issue; the material legal objection must actually be presented.

The lower court's findings about practical effectiveness and geography, read with the trial, independently sustain the judgment. Competing testimony addressed marginal districts, turnout and the proposed electorate; selected victories in Plan 1099C did not conclusively establish performance of every district in Plan 1123C. The geographic dispute included corridors, community relationships and subdivision boundaries, not shape alone. Comparative scores were relevant but did not compel a finding of workable geography. The findings are brief, yet identify grounds sufficient for review without adopting every appellate characterization of the testimony. The separate multigroup-cohesion finding does not answer a single-group crossover claim. Neither partisan convenience nor statewide gains could excuse proved local deprivation; the required violation was not established on the independent grounds here. Plan 1151C and the 2002 election under it remain undisturbed.

**O'Connor, concurring in the judgment; Kennedy joins Part I only.** Part I regards the trial court's practical-effectiveness and geography findings as sufficient when read with the record. The competing testimony concerns whether the additional arrangement would yield actual electoral opportunity, including marginality, turnout and the proposed electorate. Registration majorities do not answer those questions, and a geometric comparison does not by itself establish lawful compactness. Rule 52(a) requires an intelligible factual basis; it does not require discussion of every exhibit. The opinion's brevity presents a serious objection, but these findings permit review and sustain the judgment. This conclusion requires neither a numerical safe harbor nor rejection of Valdespino. Voinovich supplies the distinction between a State's choices and a federally compelled district; De Grandy supplies the need for proof beyond the necessary conditions.

In Part II, O'Connor also accepts the lower court's contextual totality determination as a sufficient basis for rejecting the requested additional district. That is an assessment of the actual opportunity shown in this record, not a conclusive defense based on statewide proportionality. Kennedy does not join this further account; his agreement is confined to the independent practical-effectiveness and geographic grounds in Part I.

**Thomas, concurring in the judgment, joined by Scalia.** Consistent with their positions in Holder v. Hall and De Grandy, they conclude that Section 2 does not authorize the asserted judicial allocation of districts to remedy this form of vote dilution. That antecedent statutory ground is sufficient to affirm. They accept the court's authority to replace an unconstitutionally malapportioned plan, but they do not join either capacity route or a finding that these plaintiffs failed the governing dilution framework. The actual electoral-performance evidence does not answer their statutory objection.

**Souter, dissenting, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer.** Part I expressly accepts the existing capacity rule and independent-ground limits stated in Stone-Zsela's Part I. Part II would vacate only the congressional rejection of the appealed additional Latino-opportunity arrangement and remand for application of that rule with sufficiently identified findings. The appeal affirmatively presents performance evidence, comparative geography and an objection to the adequacy of the findings. Its majority-registration framing does not eliminate the need to determine what the court decided about that evidence under current law.

The lower opinion links its conclusions to a majority-citizenship requirement and does not sufficiently explain why the proposed arrangement fails independently. The appellees' account of marginality and geography could support a lawful judgment, but cannot substitute for identifiable trial findings. Four selected victories do not prove dependable capacity, and a compactness score does not prove legal compactness; neither can be treated as absent. Plan 1099C testimony and Plan 1123C data must remain distinct. De Grandy requires an independent totality judgment, not repetition of a disputed capacity conclusion. The separate finding about cohesion between protected groups does not dispose of single-group outside support. The proposed remand would preserve supported findings and plaintiffs' burden, require explanation of any sufficient independent ground, and permit supplementation only under ordinary procedural rules. It would neither mandate an additional district nor reopen the entire trial or disturb the 2002 election under the operative plan.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#balderas-v-texas-summary-2002-06-17).

### Carey v. Saffold — 2002-06-17

**Kennedy, dissenting, joined by Scalia and Thomas.** Section 2244(d)(2) protects a pending application. Once a California original application is finally denied, a later petition initiating another original proceeding has not yet been filed during the gap. Artuz's application-level distinction and Duncan's attention to statutory categories support that reading. An actual timely appeal may continue disposition of the original application; California's later original petition is different. Functional resemblance and the policy favoring exhaustion cannot make a future application already pending. The dissent accepts that the State's selected procedure may consequently affect the federal calculation but regards that consequence as the enacted distinction. It agrees that an alternative merits ruling does not establish timely filing. It would reverse the interval-exclusion rule and return for the actual calculation, preserving independently available timing grounds on their own predicates.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#carey-v-saffold-merits-2002-06-17).

### Leventhal v. Knapek — 2002-06-17

**Scalia, concurring in part and in the judgment.** He joins retained privacy and the actual reasonableness determination, but not a general account making workplace privacy depend on unrestricted case-by-case managerial balancing. His Ortega concurrence protects government offices while allowing ordinary, reasonable searches that a private employer could undertake to retrieve work material or investigate workplace-rule violations. These confined program inspections meet that rule. They require neither police powers nor fictional blanket employee consent.

**Stevens, concurring in part and in the judgment.** He joins retained privacy but maintains the objection to deliberate warrantless evidentiary investigation that he adopted in the Ortega dissent. The indirect complaint and subsequent inspections do not satisfy that view. He nevertheless affirms the personal judgment under Guest's governing objective immunity rule: the opposing Ortega plurality and concurrence supplied substantial support for such reasonable workplace investigations, so their unlawfulness was not clearly established in 1996–1997. His contrary constitutional view did not establish binding notice. Harlow, Anderson, Elder and Wilson require the actual conduct-date inquiry, not proof that the officers personally consulted precise authorizing precedent. Later privacy decisions supply no retrospective notice. This personal defense supplies no immunity to an institution or merits approval of every search.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#leventhal-v-knapek-merits-2002-06-17).

### Ticknor v. Choice Hotels International, Inc. — 2002-06-17

**O'Connor, dissenting, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas.** They accept FAA coverage, genuinely general defenses and Montana's complete conflicts rule. They disagree with the application. Supported nonnegotiability is not synonymous with oppression, and the agreement reserves only indemnification, money-collection and trademark-injunction claims, which may reflect distinct obligations. Choice remains bound to arbitrate other disputes. The commercial alternatives, actual expectations and practical effect require fuller and more accurate legal assessment. Casarotto does not invalidate every asymmetric clause, and First Options preserves clear-error review of historical findings rather than allowing an appellate assumption that addenda prove negotiation. Green Tree's insistence on a demonstrated impediment supports scrutiny of the actual defense, not categorical enforcement. They would remand for accurate, evenhanded conflicts and enforceability analysis, retaining supported findings and independent party limits; they would not compel automatic arbitration of every claim or guarantor.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#ticknor-v-choice-hotels-international-inc-merits-2002-06-17).

### United States v. Drayton — 2002-06-17

**Souter, dissenting, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens and Ginsburg.** They accept Bostick's objective totality inquiry and reject any indispensable refusal warning. They disagree about the combined message. An officer facing passengers from the front, two colleagues moving from the rear, close requests to seated passengers and announcement of an interdiction operation communicated expected submission before travel resumed. The driver's absence and travel arrangements supplied context, not police seizure by themselves. Quiet voices, concealed weapons and a passable aisle weigh for the Government but do not overcome that coordinated display. Both respondents were already subject to it before Brown's arrest; the arrest intensified the pressure on Drayton. No physical blockade or express command was necessary. Without an adequate independent justification, the continuous restraint was unlawful, and no release or intervening event separated the immediate searches from it. They would preserve suppression on that taint ground and return for the consequences appropriate to Brown's conditional plea and Drayton's trial conviction, without acquittal, an independent consent holding or a new Terry theory.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#united-states-v-drayton-merits-2002-06-17).

### United States v. Emerson — 2002-06-17

**Stone-Zsela, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** The Chief Justice joins individual coverage and the statutory alternatives. An actual civil danger adjudication may suffice without a criminal conviction or a particular formula, but complete private-possession disarmament burdens the core of the right. He would overrule Napier's intermediate-scrutiny classification to the extent it avoids strict scrutiny for that complete deprivation. Wright, in his view, requires a compelling protective interest, narrow tailoring and no comparably effective, materially less restrictive alternative.

Preventing lethal domestic violence is compelling. The hearing, specific threat, bodily-injury restraint and Texas protective standard matter, but do not conclusively establish the fit of every-possession prohibition. He would return the constitutional application for assessment of actual individualized risk, duration, review mechanisms and supported narrower conduct or access restrictions. Such alternatives may be inadequate if continued firearm access preserves the danger; that must be assessed rather than assumed. Existing materials may suffice, with additional proceedings only if needed. If the Government makes the required showing the prosecution may proceed subject to all elements; otherwise the unconstitutional application must be dismissed. The remand would not automatically retry the divorce dispute. He agrees in the judgment rejecting the independent Fifth Amendment dismissal ground without joining its reasoning.

**Scalia, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Thomas.** They join coverage, the statutory alternatives and the fair-warning holding. Their Napier position requires actual justification of the full possession burden even where a civil order lawfully restrains violence. The hearing and Texas protective standard support a serious affirmance argument, but do not themselves complete that federal constitutional inquiry. They would vacate the arms application for restriction-specific review under Wright and Napier, considering the actual protective basis and operation of the entire disability. They do not require completed violence, a criminal conviction or the (C)(i) words, and do not adopt a rule that every individual restriction requires strict scrutiny. Nor do they find the hearing defective, disbelieve the wife or require automatic additional evidence. Ordinary further proceedings are permitted as needed; their proposed return is not immediate dismissal, acquittal or firearm restoration.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#united-states-v-emerson-merits-2002-06-17).

### United States v. Fior D'Italia, Inc. — 2002-06-17

**Souter, dissenting, joined by Scalia and Thomas.** Section 6201 authorizes assessment of the tax Congress imposed; it does not replace the employee-based taxable wages specified by Section 3121(q) with restaurant-wide receipts. The monthly exclusion, annual bases and notice-and-demand structure require determinations tied to the recipient before the employer's liability is computed from them. Estimates of particular employees' taxable wages remain possible. The dissent reserves whether a formal employee audit must precede the employer action; it does not adopt a collection or benefits-credit prerequisite. Accurate aggregate factual estimates do not resolve the disputed legal method, so the restaurant's stipulation does not concede authority. Cleveland Indians separates the applicable annual tax limits from other functions, while United Dominion's aggregation followed that different statutory scheme. The dissent would affirm the refund judgment on the employee-based determination ground, without finding numerical error or an abusive motive.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#united-states-v-fior-ditalia-inc-merits-2002-06-17).

### United States v. Hooper — 2002-06-17

No separate opinion is filed.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#united-states-v-hooper-merits-2002-06-17).

### Atkins v. Virginia — 2002-06-20

**Scalia, dissenting, joined by Thomas.** The domestic record does not establish the objective constitutional rejection required to overturn Penry. Fewer than half of capital-punishment States adopted the asserted rule, several enactments are recent or prospective, and their exceptions and definitions matter. States without capital punishment do not independently decide which offenders may be executed. The majority's independent penal judgment replaces legislative choice and juries' individual assessment; serious crimes and variation within the clinical class weaken a categorical culpability conclusion. Polling, foreign practice and professional submissions neither establish the controlling domestic legal standard nor provide a tested substitute. The dissent would preserve individualized mitigation and affirm against this categorical challenge; it does not resolve the contested diagnosis.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#atkins-v-virginia-merits-2002-06-20).

### Christopher v. Harbury — 2002-06-20

**Thomas, concurring in the judgment.** The Constitution does not impose the information duty asserted here: officials need not provide national-security information or answer informal requests in the claimed manner. That narrower objection suffices to reject this access count. The affirmative practical-assistance obligation addressed in Lewis concerned persons in custody and does not create this outside-custody duty. Thomas does not join the Court's assumed-right framework or its two alternative analyses, and does not assert a general license to obstruct actual judicial proceedings.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#christopher-v-harbury-merits-2002-06-20).

### City of Columbus v. Ours Garage & Wrecker Service, Inc. — 2002-06-20

**Scalia, dissenting, joined by O'Connor.** Congress expressly named States and political subdivisions in the broad prohibition and in selected exceptions but named only the State in this safety reservation. That difference should control. Mortier involved a statute lacking the same express institutional contrast, so ordinary delegation cannot erase the choice made here. The dissent would affirm the categorical exclusion of independent municipal regulations under this exception. It does not forbid local enforcement of valid state rules or purport to invalidate every generally applicable traffic law.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#city-of-columbus-v-ours-garage-wrecker-service-inc-merits-2002-06-20).

### Dadian v. Village of Wilmette — 2002-06-20

No separate opinion is filed.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#dadian-v-village-of-wilmette-merits-2002-06-20).

### Gonzaga University v. Doe — 2002-06-20

**Breyer, concurring in the judgment, joined by Souter.** This statute's broad education-record coverage, uncertain reach in practical administration and institutional policy-or-practice structure do not establish the asserted judicially enforceable privacy entitlement. That FERPA-specific conclusion is sufficient. A new general demand for language proving intent to authorize a private remedy would blur the separate question whether Congress already supplied enforcement through Section 1983. Administrative consistency informs the interpretation of this particular obligation; it does not independently extinguish a right already found.

**Stone-Zsela, dissenting, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg.** FERPA identifies covered records, persons whose consent controls and authorized disclosures. Under Blessing, those terms define a manageable personal privacy entitlement. The policy-or-practice condition limits the violation; it does not make the student merely an incidental beneficiary. The protected education records contain information directly related to a student and are maintained by the institution or a person acting for it. Consent rights pass from the parent to the student upon reaching eighteen or attending a postsecondary institution. A claimant must still establish the required institutional policy or practice and an unauthorized disclosure under the enacted exceptions, together with Section 1983's independent predicates; the dissent does not decide any disputed statutory violation beyond the granted enforceability issue. Central correction and funding sanctions neither expressly exclude that channel nor supply an incompatible individual remedial substitute. Unlike Powell's regulation-created claim, the asserted entitlement arises from the statute. Sandoval addresses a different direct enforcement route; the absence of an implied FERPA action does not defeat Congress's existing Section 1983 remedy. State action is assumed solely within the grant limit. The dissent would affirm the federal enforceability ruling without adding damages or reopening state-law awards.

**The four-Justice lead opinion.** Thomas, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, reads FERPA's policy-or-practice funding condition as an institutional obligation administered through federal oversight rather than the asserted individual nondisclosure entitlement. Mandatory wording, identified records and consent instructions do not alone change that statutory object. Blessing requires a particular individual right; Bruneau preserves Section 1983 once that right is established but does not create it here. This analysis does not make funding statutes categorically unenforceable, treat absence of an implied action as dispositive, or establish automatic administrative foreclosure. Breyer and Souter agree with the judgment under their narrower explanation and do not join the lead opinion.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#gonzaga-university-v-doe-merits-2002-06-20).

### Page v. Bartels — 2002-06-20

**Thomas, concurring in part and in the judgment, joined by Scalia.** They agree that the reviewed denial of relief should be affirmed and join the constitutional dispositions. They would reject the statutory district-allocation claim because Section 2 does not authorize that asserted entitlement. Their Valdespino and Balderas positions limit the statute to voting access, casting and counting rather than judicial allocation of districts. Conditional acceptance that ordinary factual review would sustain the judgment if the Gingles framework applied is not a direct join in the Court's statutory analysis or either capacity route. Their separate statutory ground does not establish a numerical-majority rule or displace the Court's governing framework.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#page-v-bartels-summary-2002-06-20).

### Robertson v. Bartels — 2002-06-20

**Kennedy, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by O'Connor, Scalia and Thomas.** They join rejection of the stated nonparty-preclusion ground but would vacate summary judgment on Counts I and II for consideration under their proposed predominant-racial-assignment rule. Following their Easley position, they regard governmental racial assignment as unequal treatment when race actually predominates, without requiring an additional electoral loss. Bartels's express minority-incumbent protection commitment permits a reasonable inference about racial priority. A regular map and the presence of other criteria do not conclusively defeat that inference on summary judgment. The proposed remand would assess the relative role of race and any required justification under ordinary procedure. It would not find predominance or a violation established, order new boundaries, or guarantee a trial. This opinion seeks reconsideration of the Court's territorial-design boundary; it does not describe that proposed rule as existing law.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#robertson-v-bartels-summary-2002-06-20).

### Rush Prudential HMO, Inc. v. Moran — 2002-06-20

**Thomas, dissenting, joined by Scalia and Kennedy.** The dissent would reverse the preemption ruling and return the benefits claim for determination through ERISA's process without treating the outside physician's decision as binding. It assumes that the State regulates insurance but concludes that the compulsory determination is an incompatible alternate remedial mechanism. Unlike the notice-prejudice condition sustained in UNUM, the reviewer determines the disputed obligation through an external adjudication that replaces the plan's decision. Pilot Life's remedial exclusivity and Great-West's insistence on the enacted enforcement boundaries remain decisive even when the subject is insured coverage. The dissent accepts ordinary saved insurance terms; its objection is the binding substitute process. It would neither decide medical necessity itself nor categorically deny Moran benefits.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#rush-prudential-hmo-inc-v-moran-merits-2002-06-20).

### Utah v. Evans — 2002-06-20

**O'Connor, concurring in part and dissenting in part, joined by Scalia.** They join the standing holding but would reverse under §195 and remand for lawful agency correction through the statutory sequence. In their view, donor information substitutes an inferred population for information never observed, and choosing donors late, nonrandomly or for only a small residual group does not remove the technique from the statutory sampling restriction. The prior permission for two other designs binds those designs but does not settle this different method. They reserve the constitutional question and would leave any corrected count and allocation to the lawful official process.

**Thomas, concurring in part and dissenting in part.** He joins standing and statutory permission, agreeing that this imputation falls outside the statutory sampling restriction. He would nevertheless reverse because the Constitution requires an actual count rather than adding persons through donor inference, especially where the existence or occupancy of the unit is unresolved. He disagrees with extending Department of Commerce's constitutional permission and with its rejection of a count-based limit to the extent necessary. His position is a dissent from that governing premise, not a description of the current rule. He would remand for lawful agency correction rather than judicially specify a replacement number.

Exact holdings, authority, precedent treatment and remedy: [public law](../entering-law/OT_2001CHUNK7_PUBLIC_LAW.md#utah-v-evans-merits-2002-06-20).

## 5. Current Procedure and Institution

All nine Justices are seated. Seniority is Stone-Zsela as Chief Justice, followed among the Associates by Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Case-specific participation is determined at argument or submission and again at decision. The opening neutral participation fields alone established no exclusion; the completed Rucker Record now states the case-specific nonparticipation established during source validation. All nine participated in every chunk 2 event, including Gulf Power. Chunk 3 participation is stated event by event below; the seated roster does not itself establish participation.

Porter v. Nussle: All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Swierkiewicz v. Sorema N.A.: All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Guest v. Leis: All nine Justices participated at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Raygor v. Regents of University of Minnesota: All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Delaney v. DeTella: All nine Justices participated at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. New York v. FERC / Enron Power Marketing, Inc. v. FERC: All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Ruiz v. United States (Ruiz v. Johnson): All nine Justices participated at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. United States v. Vonn: All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Young v. United States: All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Edelman v. Lynchburg College: All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Milstein v. Cooley: All nine Justices participated at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Ragsdale v. Wolverine World Wide, Inc.: All nine Justices participated at argument and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Zahrey v. Coffey: All nine Justices participated at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Department of Housing and Urban Development v. Rucker / Oakland Housing Authority v. Rucker: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg participated. Breyer took no part in the consideration or decision of these cases. S.S. ex rel. Jervis v. McMullen: All nine Justices participated at submission and decision: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. Hoffman Plastic Compounds, Inc. v. NLRB: All nine Justices participated: Stone-Zsela, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer. The three chunk 5 participation flags are resolved in the completed Records: all nine Justices participated in Verizon Communications, Verizon Maryland and Mathias. The [manifest](manifest.md#participation-flags-for-run) preserves the inventory flags without importing an exclusion.

The August 3, 1994 allotment order remains in force: Stone-Zsela—District of Columbia, Fourth and Federal; Breyer—First; Ginsburg—Second; Souter—Third; Scalia—Fifth; Stevens—Sixth and Seventh; O'Connor—Eighth and Tenth; Kennedy—Ninth; Thomas—Eleventh. The Court's standing referral, signed-explanation and certification practice remains as stated in Standing State section 3.

**Referral of applications, effective October 7, 1991; signed explanations and certification, effective October 2, 1995.** By the Court's internal practice adopted at the opening of October Term 1991, an application for a stay, injunction, or other interim relief in a matter on the Term's inventory is referred by the Circuit Justice to the full Court and decided by the participating Justices under the participating-majority rule. The order issues in the Court's name and recites the presenting Circuit Justice ("presented to Justice ___ and by him or her referred to the Court"). From October 2, 1995, referral does not prevent the Circuit Justice, or the substitute the term-effective Rules designate, from taking a necessary temporary action in his or her own name while the Court considers the application. Each action is explained in short signed writings, including the Circuit Justice's own explanation at final disposition, whether prevailing or dissenting, and a statement by each participating Justice in each Court action deciding relief, and is certified for issuance by the Chief Justice or the Chief's substitute, as Engine §10 provides. A Justice may participate in a referred application from the date of the judicial oath. An application not on the Term's inventory remains subject to ordinary Circuit Justice practice. Source: the OT1991 opening practice carried in the opening Standing State and the OT1993 Term Continuity Note. Source of the October 2, 1995 revision: user instruction.

California*, No. 8, Original | Supplemental decree in force; original jurisdiction retained; unresolved Fort Yuma title, cession, boundary, irrigable-acreage and water-quantity issues before the Special Master. | No OT2001 event date is scheduled. | Carryover; original action remains open. |
| *Kansas v. Colorado*, No. 105, Original | Recommitted to the Special Master for damages calculation and preparation of judgment; original action pending. | No OT2001 event date is scheduled. | Carryover; original action remains open. |

These stages are copied from Standing State section 4. No report, filing, decree, or other OT2001 event is scheduled or invented for either action. They remain open until a dated terminal event or an expressly authorized continuity closure resolves them.

### Continuing procedure in Nebraska v. Wyoming

The specified claims are concluded with prejudice. Decree administration continues under paragraphs XIII and XVIII, with Court jurisdiction retained. Further litigation costs will be allocated by subsequent order; paragraph XIV preserves the confirmed prior apportionments. The Clerk transmits authenticated copies as directed by XV.

No new event date is scheduled for retained decree administration or further costs. The settled claims and continuing administrative jurisdiction retain the different statuses stated in the Record. Other completed-event stages appear in block 2.

Romeu v. Cohen returns through the Second Circuit for the prospective declaration, injunction and ordinary eligibility determinations stated in its February 19 Record. Its equality holdings and published positions enter later cases only at their actual scope and effective date.
